<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove the Gaussian propeller conjecture: for every finite measurable partition of a Euclidean space, the sum of the squared lengths of its Gaussian first moments is at most $`9/(8\pi)`$. For dimension at least two and at least three cells, three planar sectors of angle $`2\pi/3`$, extended by an orthogonal Euclidean factor, attain the bound.

- **claim-main-bound** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For all positive integers $d$ and $k$, every measurable partition $(A_1,\ldots,A_k)$ of $\mathbb R^d$ satisfies $$\sum_{i=1}^k\left\\|\int_{A_i}x\,d\gamma_d(x)\right\\|^2 \le\frac9{8\pi}.$$ Empty cells are allowed. The constant is sharp whenever $d\ge2$ and $k\ge3$: three planar sectors of angle $2\pi/3$, multiplied by the orthogonal complement of their plane, attain equality, with any further cells empty.
  - Explanation: Every finite measurable partition of Euclidean space has total squared Gaussian first-moment length below the stated constant. Three planar sectors extended by an orthogonal factor attain it when the dimension and the number of cells are large enough.
- **claim-extremal-duality** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ext-duality)): If $g$ is a standard Gaussian vector in $\mathbb R^n$, then $$\label{ext:dual-formula} \sqrt C= \max_{\sum_{i=1}^N\\|v_i\\|^2=1} \mathbb E\max_{1\le i\le N}\langle v_i,g\rangle.$$ The supremum defining $C$ is attained. Moreover, for every $1\le k\le N$ and every list $v_1,\ldots,v_k\in\mathbb R^n$, $$\label{ext:shorter-list} \mathbb E\max_{1\le i\le k}\langle v_i,g\rangle \le \sqrt C\left(\sum_{i=1}^k\\|v_i\\|^2\right)^{1/2}.$$
  - Explanation: The square root of the supremum equals the largest expected maximum of Gaussian scores over lists of vectors with unit total squared norm. The supremum is attained, and shorter lists obey the same bound.
- **claim-centroid-structure** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ext-structure)): The centroids satisfy $\langle z_i,z_j\rangle<0$ for $i\ne j$. Their span $V$ has dimension $m-1$, every proper subset of the centroids is linearly independent, and, for each fixed $i$, the $m-1$ vectors $z_i-z_j$, $j\ne i$, form a basis of $V$. The optimal cells have the closed conical representatives $$\label{ext:cones} K_i=\{x\in\mathbb R^n: \langle z_i-z_j,x\rangle\ge0\text{ for every }j\ne i\}.$$ Furthermore, $m\ge5$.
  - Explanation: For an optimal partition with fewest active cells, centroids have strictly negative pairwise inner products and span a space one dimension below their count. The cells are the closed cones where the corresponding score is largest, and at least five cells are active.
- **claim-cell-probability-bounds** (bound, [source](#stmt-sc-bounds)): Set $$A=\begin{cases}0.929,&m=5,\\0.884,&m\ge6.\end{cases}$$ For every $i$, $$\label{sc:three-bounds} r_i\le\frac23,\qquad P_i\ge Ar_i^2,\qquad P_i\ge0.415r_i+0.15r_i^2.$$
  - Explanation: Each normalized centroid length is capped, and each cell probability is bounded below by a quadratic and by a linear-quadratic expression in that length, with the quadratic coefficient depending on whether five or more cells are active.
- **claim-truncated-variance-monotone** (theorem, [source](#stmt-sc-truncated-variance)): The function $G$ is nonincreasing and satisfies $0<G(q)<1$. Moreover, $$P\longmapsto P^2G\bigl(\Phi^{-1}(P)\bigr)$$ is increasing on $(0,1/2]$.
  - Explanation: The truncated-variance ratio function is nonincreasing with values strictly between zero and one, and the associated probability expression is increasing, which lets the implicit loss constraint be turned into an explicit lower bound.
- **claim-two-competing-scores** (bound, [source](#stmt-geo-pair)): For every pair of distinct indices $j,k<m$, $$D_{jk}\le \frac3\pi\,t(1+a_j)(1+a_k). \label{geo:pair-bound}$$
  - Explanation: For any two residual scores, the square root of their covariance determinant is bounded by a multiple of the smallest radius, obtained by bounding a positive-part integral over a triangle with the maximal density.
- **claim-three-radii-constraints** (bound, [source](#stmt-geo-constraints)): The quantities $L,c,t$ satisfy $$\begin{aligned} (c-t^2)(L-c-2t^2) &\le8\left(\frac3\pi\right)^2t^2, \label{geo:first-constraint}\\ \frac32(c-t^2)^2 &\le10\left(\frac3\pi\right)^2t^2. \label{geo:second-constraint} \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: Two algebraic inequalities tie the three largest radii to the smallest radius, derived by summing the pairwise determinant bounds over the three largest competitors.
- **claim-gaussian-maxima** (bound, [source](#stmt-cor-gaussian-maxima)): Let $d,k$ be positive integers, let $g$ be standard Gaussian in $\mathbb R^d$, and let $v_1,\ldots,v_k\in\mathbb R^d$, with $\bar v=k^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^k v_i$. Then $$\mathbb E\max_{1\le i\le k}\langle v_i,g\rangle \le\frac{3}{2\sqrt{2\pi}} \left(\sum_{i=1}^k\\|v_i-\bar v\\|^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ The constant is sharp already for three vectors in the plane.
  - Explanation: The expected maximum of finitely many Gaussian scores is bounded by the sharp constant times the root of the sum of squared deviations from the mean vector; the planar propeller shows sharpness.
- **claim-kc-hardness** (theorem, [source](#stmt-kc-main)): For each fixed $k\ge3$ and every fixed $1\le\alpha<\alpha_k$, it is NP-hard, given a rational symmetric matrix $A\succeq0$ with $A\mathbf 1=0$ and the identity-target objective <a href="#kc:value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:value">[kc:value]</a>, to find $\sigma:[N]\to[k]$ such that $$\operatorname{val}_A(\sigma)\ge\operatorname{Clust}(A\mid I_k)/\alpha.$$ In particular, such a deterministic polynomial-time approximation would imply $\mathrm P=\mathrm{NP}$.
  - Explanation: For a fixed number of clusters at least three, finding an assignment within any fixed loss factor strictly below the threshold is NP-hard on rational centered positive semidefinite inputs.
- **claim-kn-low-influence** (theorem, [source](#stmt-kc-kn-input)): For fixed $k$ and every $\eta>0$, there exists $0<\tau_0<1/2$, independent of $r$, such that every $r\in\mathbb N$ and every $f:\Omega^r\to\Delta_k$ satisfying $$\operatorname{Inf}_j^{\le\log(1/\tau_0)}(f_i)\le\tau_0 \quad(i\in[k],\ j\in[r])$$ obey $\operatorname{OBJ}(f)\le C_k+\eta$.
  - Explanation: Imported discrete bound: simplex-valued functions on a product space with small low-degree influences have objective at most the Gaussian constant plus a margin.
- **claim-ug-input** (theorem, [source](#stmt-kc-ug-input)): For every fixed $\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1/2)$, there are an integer $s\ge1$ and a deterministic polynomial-time reduction from $\mathrm{3SAT}$ to explicit unweighted simple bipartite Unique Games instances over $\mathbb F_2^s$. If the formula is satisfiable the game has a labeling satisfying at least $1-\varepsilon$ of its edges; if it is unsatisfiable every labeling satisfies at most $\delta$ of its edges. The edge set is nonempty and each constraint is a translation of the alphabet. The alphabet size and the polynomial’s degree and constants depend only on $\varepsilon,\delta$.
  - Explanation: Imported interface: for any small error parameters there is a deterministic polynomial-time reduction from 3SAT to unweighted bipartite Unique Games instances with translation constraints and a completeness and soundness gap.
- **claim-kc-matrix** (construction, [source](#stmt-kc-matrix)): For fixed $k,r$, one can construct in deterministic polynomial time a rational symmetric matrix $A$, indexed by $W\times\Omega^r$, such that $$h^{\mathsf T}Ah=Q(h),\qquad A\succeq0,\qquad A\mathbf 1=0 .$$ For every $\sigma:W\times\Omega^r\to[k]$, if $F_i(w,x)=\mathbf 1_{\{\sigma(w,x)=i\}}$, then $$\label{kc:objective-identity} \operatorname{val}_A(\sigma)= \sum_{v\in V}\mu(v)\operatorname{OBJ}(F_v),\qquad F_v=(T_vF_1,\ldots,T_vF_k):\Omega^r\to\Delta_k.$$ All output assignment values lie in $[0,1]$.
  - Explanation: A rational symmetric centered positive semidefinite matrix is built in deterministic polynomial time whose clustering value equals the weighted average of the discrete objective of neighbor-averaged indicator tables.
- **claim-kc-completeness** (bound, [source](#stmt-kc-completeness)): If a labeling of $G$ satisfies at least $1-\varepsilon$ of its edges, then the matrix of Lemma <a href="#kc:matrix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kc:matrix">12</a> satisfies $$\operatorname{Clust}(A\mid I_k)\ge \left(1-\frac1k\right)(1-\varepsilon)^2.$$
  - Explanation: A labeling satisfying most edges yields a clustering value at least a fixed fraction, namely the square of the satisfied fraction times the cluster factor.
- **claim-kc-soundness** (bound, [source](#stmt-kc-soundness)): Fix $\eta>0$ with $C_k+2\eta<1$, and choose $\tau,D$ as in <a href="#kc:cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:cutoff">[kc:cutoff]</a> for this $\eta$. If every labeling of $G$ satisfies at most $\delta$ of its edges, where $$\label{kc:delta} 0<\delta<\frac{\eta\tau^2}{4kD},$$ then $\operatorname{Clust}(A\mid I_k)\le C_k+2\eta$.
  - Explanation: If no labeling satisfies more than a tiny fraction of edges, the clustering value stays below the Gaussian constant plus a margin, by decoding influential coordinates into short label lists.
- **remark-randomized** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-kc-randomized)): The quoted Khot–Naor rounding guarantee is in expectation. The deterministic hardness conclusion of Theorem <a href="#kc:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kc:main">9</a> does not use $\mathrm P\ne\mathrm{NP}$ to exclude randomized algorithms. More precisely, a polynomial-time randomized algorithm whose expected value is at least $\operatorname{Clust}(A\mid I_k)/\alpha$ for some fixed $\alpha<\alpha_k$ would place $\mathrm{3SAT}$ in $\mathrm{RP}$. Indeed, on the reduction instances its returned value $X$ lies in $[0,1]$. In the satisfiable case $\mathbb EX\ge H/\alpha>\theta$; therefore $$\Pr\{X>\theta\}\ge \frac{H/\alpha-\theta}{1-\theta}>0.$$ This is a fixed positive constant. In the unsatisfiable case $X\le S<\theta$ always. A fixed number of repetitions gives one-sided success probability at least $1/2$.
  - Explanation: Hardness is deterministic, but a randomized polynomial-time algorithm with expected loss factor below the threshold would place 3SAT in RP.
- **remark-dependencies** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-kc-dependencies)): The Gaussian proof supplies only <a href="#kc:gaussian-constant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:gaussian-constant">[kc:gaussian-constant]</a>. Khot and Naor supply the expected rounding guarantee [[KhotNaor](#ref-KhotNaor), Theorem 2.1] and the analytic low-influence bound [[KhotNaor](#ref-KhotNaor), Theorem 3.8]. Their reduction in Section 3.3 is the model for Lemmas <a href="#kc:matrix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kc:matrix">12</a>–<a href="#kc:soundness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kc:soundness">14</a>; the degree weights and the squared completeness loss explicitly accommodate the ordinary edge-value, possibly nonregular instances from [[UniqueGamesTheorem](#ref-UniqueGamesTheorem), Theorem 1.1]. No stronger Unique Games promise is assumed. The separate Unique Games theorem is a formal dependency only of this hardness application. The algorithmic endpoint is quoted in its ideal rounding formulation; this section supplies no finite-precision endpoint implementation.
  - Explanation: The Gaussian proof supplies only the constant; the rounding guarantee, low-influence bound and Unique Games theorem are external inputs to the hardness application.

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Gaussian first moment** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For a measurable set $A\subseteq\mathbb R^n$, its Gaussian first moment is $z(A)=\int_A x\,d\gamma_n(x)$.
- **centroid** ([source](#sec:introduction)): We call this unnormalized vector its *centroid*.
- **partition functional** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For positive integers $d,k$ and a measurable partition $\mathcal A=(A_1,\ldots,A_k)$ of $\mathbb R^d$, define $$\mathcal F(\mathcal A)=\sum_{i=1}^k\\|z(A_i)\\|^2.$$
- **active cell** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Here an active cell has positive Gaussian measure.
- **supremum C and constant B** ([source](#ext:section)): Write $$C=\sup_{(A_1,\ldots,A_N)} \sum_{i=1}^N\left\\|\int_{A_i}x\,d\gamma_n(x)\right\\|^2, \qquad B=\frac{3}{2\sqrt{2\pi}}.$$
- **Gaussian density, distribution and normalized quantities** ([source](#sc:section)): Write $$\phi(x)=\frac{e^{-x^2/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}},\qquad \Phi(x)=\int_{-\infty}^x\phi(u)\,du,\qquad r_i=\frac{\|z_i\|}{\sqrt C},\qquad P_i=\gamma_{m-1}(K_i).$$
- **truncated-variance function G** ([source](#sc:loss-upper)): Define $$G(q)=\frac{\phi(q)\bigl(q\Phi(q)+\phi(q)\bigr)}{\Phi(q)^2}.$$
- **kernel-clustering objective** ([source](#kc:value)): For a map $\sigma:[N]\to[k]$, define $$\label{kc:value} \operatorname{val}_A(\sigma)=\sum_{p,q=1}^N a_{pq}\mathbf 1_{\{\sigma(p)=\sigma(q)\}}, \qquad \operatorname{Clust}(A\mid I_k)=\max_{\sigma:[N]\to[k]}\operatorname{val}_A(\sigma).$$
- **loss factor** ([source](#pm-h-19)): A deterministic approximation with *loss factor* $\alpha\ge1$ returns an assignment of value at least $\operatorname{Clust}(A\mid I_k)/\alpha$.
- **Gaussian constant and threshold** ([source](#pm-h-19)): Put $$c_*=\frac9{8\pi}, \qquad \alpha_k=\frac{1-1/k}{c_*} =\frac{8\pi}{9}\left(1-\frac1k\right).$$
- **simplex with slack** ([source](#kc:influence)): Define the closed simplex with slack by $$\Delta_k=\{u\in\mathbb R^k:u_i\ge0,\ \sum_{i=1}^k u_i\le1\}.$$

| q-main-bound | sum of squared Gaussian first-moment lengths over a finite measurable partition | ≤ | $\frac9{8\pi}$ | all positive dimensions and cell counts |
| q-propeller-value | value attained by three planar sectors | = | $9/(8\pi)$ | dimension at least two, at least three cells |
| q-constant-B | square root of the conjectured bound | = | $\frac{3}{2\sqrt{2\pi}}$ | dimension at least four |
| q-half-probability | cell probability | ≤ | $\frac12$ | every active cell |
| q-radius-cap | normalized centroid length | ≤ | $\frac23$ | every active cell |
| q-quadratic-probability | cell probability | ≥ | $Ar_i^2$ | coefficient depends on whether exactly five or at least six cells are active |
| q-quadratic-coefficient-five | coefficient A for five active cells | = | $0.929$ | five active cells |
| q-quadratic-coefficient-six | coefficient A for six or more active cells | = | $0.884$ | six or more active cells |
| q-linear-quadratic-probability | cell probability | ≥ | $0.415r_i+0.15r_i^2$ | every active cell |
| q-pair-determinant | residual covariance determinant root D_{jk} | ≤ | $\frac3\pi\,t(1+a_j)(1+a_k)$ | distinct indices below the last |
| q-first-constraint | product of the third-radius excess and the remaining-mass excess | ≤ | $8(3/\pi)^2t^2$ | three largest radii and the smallest |
| q-second-constraint | three halves times squared difference of third and smallest squared radii | ≤ | $10\left(\frac3\pi\right)^2t^2$ | three largest radii and the smallest |
| q-third-radius-lower | square root of c | > | $.23$ | five or more active cells |
| q-minimum-radius-lower | smallest normalized radius t | > | $.066$ | five or more active cells |
| q-tail-radius-cap | radii from the fourth index on | ≤ | $.42$ | indices at least four |
| q-minimum-radius-upper | smallest radius t | < | $.12$ | both values of the quadratic coefficient |
| q-third-versus-minimum | third squared radius c | > | $2.6t$ | five or more active cells |
| q-gaussian-maxima-constant | multiplier in the expected-maximum bound | ≤ | $\frac{3}{2\sqrt{2\pi}}$ | all positive dimensions and counts |
| q-threshold-alpha | approximation threshold alpha_k | = | $\frac{8\pi}{9}\left(1-\frac1k\right)$ | fixed number of clusters at least three |
| q-partition-parameter | Gaussian partition parameter C_k | = | $c_*$ | number of clusters at least three |
| q-label-list-size | size of the label list at a right vertex | ≤ | $\frac{2kD}{\tau}$ | fixed cluster number and cutoff |
| q-completeness-value | optimal clustering value in the satisfiable case | ≥ | $\left(1-\frac1k\right)(1-\varepsilon)^2$ | fixed error and cluster number |
| q-soundness-value | optimal clustering value in the unsatisfiable case | ≤ | $C_k+2\eta$ | small soundness error |
| q-soundness-error | soundness error delta | < | $\frac{\eta\tau^2}{4kD}$ | fixed eta, tau, D, k |
| q-pi-lower | pi | > | $3.14159$ | global constant |
| q-pi-upper | pi | < | $3.14160$ | global constant |

          "anchor": "stmt-thm-main",
          "exact_quote": "For all positive integers $d$ and $k$, every measurable partition $(A_1,\\ldots,A_k)$ of $\\mathbb R^d$ satisfies $$\\sum_{i=1}^k\\left\\|\\int_{A_i}x\\,d\\gamma_d(x)\\right\\|^2\n \\le\\frac9{8\\pi}.$$ Empty cells are allowed. The constant is sharp whenever $d\\ge2$ and $k\\ge3$: three planar sectors of angle $2\\pi/3$, multiplied by the orthogonal complement of their plane, attain equality, with any further cells empty.",

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **s-dimension-reduction** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#ext:section): Pad the space with an orthogonal factor and append empty cells so that one more cell than the dimension is used. -> reduction to one more cell than the dimension, dimension at least four
- **s-duality** [claim](#stmt-ext-duality) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ext-duality): Euclidean norm duality plus compactness of the sphere of lists. -> dual formula, attainment, shorter-list bound
- **s-centroid-structure** [claim](#stmt-ext-structure) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ext-structure): Merge two cells and use minimality; Gram matrix with vanishing row sums gives rank. -> negative inner products, span dimension, conical cells
- **s-small-cell-exclusion** [claim](#stmt-ext-structure) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ext-structure): Embed a low-dimensional configuration in three dimensions and apply the three-dimensional propeller theorem. -> at least five active cells
- **s-rearrangement** [claim](#stmt-sc-bounds) / [proof](#sc:halfspace-ratio): Halfspace rearrangement, improved by spherical-cap rearrangement for five cells. -> radius cap and quadratic probability bound
- **s-loss-lower** [claim](#stmt-sc-bounds) / [proof](#sc:loss-lower): Delete one score, recenter, and apply the extremal dual bound. -> lower bound for the deletion loss
- **s-loss-upper** [claim](#stmt-sc-bounds) / [proof](#sc:loss-upper): Translate the winning cone and use concavity from Ehrhard's inequality with the supporting tangent. -> upper bound for the deletion loss
- **s-truncated-variance** [claim](#stmt-sc-truncated-variance) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sc-truncated-variance): Log-concavity of the conditioned survival function gives a variance comparison and monotonicity. -> monotonicity and range of G
- **s-interval-verification** [claim](#stmt-sc-bounds) / [proof](#sc:test-probability): Finite interval cover with certified rational enclosures. -> linear-quadratic probability bound
- **s-pair-bound** [claim](#stmt-geo-pair) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geo-pair): Orthogonal decomposition against the smallest centroid, then bound a positive-part integral over a triangle by the maximal density. -> pairwise determinant bound
- **s-three-radii** [claim](#stmt-geo-constraints) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geo-constraints): Sum pairwise determinant bounds and bound them below using row sums and correlations. -> two inequalities for the three largest radii
- **s-third-radius** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#elim:third-radius): Charge probability excesses and compare numerically. -> lower bound for the third radius
- **s-minimum-lower** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#elim:first-constraint): Assume a small minimum and contradict the first constraint by interval cases. -> lower bound for the smallest radius
- **s-tail-cap** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#elim:tail-cap): Clipped charge argument on the first four radii. -> cap on radii from the fourth on
- **s-minimum-upper** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#elim:tzero): Solve the quadratic budget inequality for its smaller root. -> upper bound for the smallest radius
- **s-final-contradiction** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#pm-h-16): Concavity endpoint check forces the third squared radius above a multiple of the smallest radius, contradicting the second constraint. -> no counterexample above the bound
- **s-completion** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geo-constraints-2): Transfer the bound to every dimension and cell count by padding. -> the main bound
- **s-attainment** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#sec:example): Explicit polar integration over three sectors and an orthogonal factor. -> equality case
- **s-gaussian-maxima** [claim](#stmt-cor-gaussian-maxima) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-gaussian-maxima): Center the vectors, form the score partition, apply Cauchy–Schwarz and the main bound. -> sharp expected-maximum inequality
- **s-kc-inputs** [claim](#stmt-kc-kn-input) / [proof](#kc:cutoff): Quote the discrete low-influence bound and adjust thresholds by monotonicity. -> contrapositive influence statement
- **s-kc-ug** [claim](#stmt-kc-ug-input) / [proof](#stmt-kc-ug-input): Quote the Unique Games reduction interface from the companion theorem. -> unique games instance with a gap
- **s-kc-matrix** [claim](#stmt-kc-matrix) / [proof](#proof-stmt-kc-matrix): Explicit rational matrix from the projection and neighbor-averaging operators. -> centered positive semidefinite matrix
- **s-kc-completeness** [claim](#stmt-kc-completeness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-kc-completeness): Dictator assignment from a good labeling and Jensen's inequality. -> completeness value
- **s-kc-soundness** [claim](#stmt-kc-soundness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-kc-soundness): Influence averaging and random list decoding. -> soundness value
- **s-kc-hardness** [claim](#stmt-kc-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-kc-soundness-2): Compose the reduction with the matrix construction and separate the two cases by a rational threshold. -> NP-hardness of the approximation
- **s-kc-randomized** [claim](#stmt-kc-randomized) / [proof](#stmt-kc-randomized): Markov-type argument on a value in the unit interval and repetition. -> 3SAT in RP
- **s-kc-dependencies** [claim](#stmt-kc-dependencies) / [proof](#stmt-kc-dependencies): Bookkeeping of external inputs. -> statement of what the application relies on

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-level-one-projection**: For fixed k and r, the level-one projection written in counting coordinates is a rational symmetric idempotent matrix on the functions on the k-ary cube of length r, given entrywise by the normalized sum over coordinates of k times the agreement indicator minus one.
- **inv-level-one-centered**: The same projection annihilates the all-ones vector, so every row of the matrix sums to zero; this is the centering used to obtain a centered positive semidefinite matrix.
- **inv-level-one-trace**: The trace of the projection equals the dimension of its range, namely r(k-1) for the level-one space spanned by single-coordinate centered functions.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-growing-r**: Treating the explicit matrix construction as polynomial when the alphabet exponent r is allowed to grow with the input. -> The matrix has k^r rows and columns, so the size is exponential in r.
- **anti-drop-normalisation**: Implementing the projection entries without the global normalising factor or without the subtraction of one inside each coordinate term. -> The resulting matrix is not idempotent and its rows do not sum to zero, so it is neither a projection nor centered.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The level-one projection matrix of the kernel-clustering reduction is an explicit finite rational object for fixed k and r, so its construction and defining properties can be computed and checked exactly.

```python
# INPUTS: two positive integers k and r on stdin, separated by whitespace.
# PRECONDITIONS: k >= 1, r >= 1, small enough that k**r rows are feasible.
# OUTPUTS: first line 'k r', then k**r lines each with k**r exact rational entries of the level-one projection.
# TIME: O(r * k^(2r)) rational operations.
# SPACE: O(k^(2r)) entries.
# ARITHMETIC: exact rationals via fractions.Fraction, no floating point.
# FAILURE: malformed input raises an exception and the run is reported as a crash.
import sys
from fractions import Fraction
from itertools import product


def main():
    k, r = map(int, sys.stdin.read().split())
    q = k ** r
    pts = list(product(range(k), repeat=r))
    out = []
    for x in pts:
        row = []
        for y in pts:
            s = sum(k * (1 if a == b else 0) - 1 for a, b in zip(x, y))
            row.append(str(Fraction(s, q)))
        out.append(" ".join(row))
    print(k, r)
    print("\n".join(out))


main()
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves an inequality and a hardness reduction and ships no library, API or runnable component. It explicitly declines to supply a finite-precision implementation of the rounding step. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The planar three-sector partition is a geometric extremal example. It has no dynamics, agents or simulation semantics, so it gives no game or simulation mechanism. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The appendix supplies enclosure formulas that a checker could follow, but the paper builds no tool and its certificates are specific to the paper's own numerical comparisons. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Lemma 12 gives a deterministic polynomial-time recipe for the rational centered positive semidefinite matrix built from a Unique Games instance, and that recipe can be coded directly. It needs an external game supplied by the cited Unique Games theorem, so the result is adaptable rather than a stand-alone algorithm. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The only structure involved is a dense indexed matrix; the paper introduces no new data structure with operations or invariants worth reusing. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No parallel, circuit or memory-hierarchy claim appears. The enclosure computations are stated as rational operations without any hardware mapping. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The NP-hardness is a worst-case approximation barrier for a clustering objective. It gives no average-case hardness, no primitive and no security reduction. |
| machine_learning | True | weak | inspirational | inferred | The identity-target kernel-clustering objective is an explicit evaluable quantity on a centered positive semidefinite matrix, and the paper fixes its best achievable approximation threshold. It informs what guarantees are possible, not a training method. |
| optimisation_operations_research | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The main result gives the exact optimum of an unrestricted partition-moment problem, and the hardness theorem shows that fixed approximation factors below the stated threshold would collapse complexity classes. Both bound what an optimiser can promise. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper concerns communication, consensus, faults or message passing; the only algorithmic notion is a sequential polynomial-time or randomized algorithm. |
| other (Gaussian extreme-value bounds) | True | moderate | implementable | inferred | Corollary 8 gives a closed-form sharp upper bound on the expected maximum of finitely many Gaussian linear scores, which can be evaluated directly from the vectors. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-sharpness-regime** (hypothesis): Equality is asserted only when the dimension is at least two and at least three cells are available. Outside that range the bound is valid, but attainment by the three-sector construction is not claimed. Blocked use: ['Claiming that the bound is attained in dimension one or with fewer than three cells', 'Using the three-sector value as the exact maximum for every dimension and cell count']
- **limit-unnormalized-centroid** (formal_scope): The functional uses unnormalized Gaussian first moments, not centroids divided by the cell measure. The bound does not transfer to objectives built from normalized cluster means. Blocked use: ['Applying the bound to clustering scores that divide each moment by the cell probability', 'Reading the bound as a statement about conditional means of cells']
- **limit-gaussian-null-sets** (formal_scope): Partitions are taken up to Gaussian null sets, with empty cells allowed and cell probabilities unrestricted. Statements about pointwise or topological properties of the cells are outside the result. Blocked use: ['Deducing boundary regularity of the cells from the bound', 'Imposing equal-mass constraints and expecting the same value']
- **limit-three-dimensional-input** (formal_scope): The exclusion of configurations with at most four active cells rests on the external three-dimensional propeller theorem, which is quoted rather than reproved. The result is conditional on that input. Blocked use: ['Presenting the five-or-more-cells estimates as a self-contained proof of the whole bound', 'Reusing the argument where the external theorem is unavailable']
- **limit-at-least-five-cells** (hypothesis): The scalar, pairwise and elimination estimates apply to a hypothetical extremizer exceeding the conjectured value with at least five active cells. They do not describe actual partitions of smaller complexity. Blocked use: ['Applying the probability lower bounds to arbitrary partitions that are not optimal', 'Using the radius bounds when fewer than five cells are active']
- **limit-extremizer-nonconstructive** (nonconstructive): Attainment of the supremum comes from compactness of the sphere of lists. No procedure for finding the maximizing list or partition is given. Blocked use: ['Reading the duality as an algorithm that computes an optimal partition', 'Assuming an effective construction of the extremizer in general dimension']
- **limit-ehrhard-convex-cone** (hypothesis): The deletion-loss upper bound uses concavity from Ehrhard's inequality for translates of a closed convex cone with nonempty interior. It does not apply to cells without that conical convex structure. Blocked use: ['Using the loss bound for arbitrary measurable cells', 'Applying the translation derivative formula to nonconvex score regions']
- **limit-numerical-certificates** (approximation): The finite numerical comparisons are certified by rational enclosures of pi, the exponential and the normal distribution. They hold only at the tabulated arguments and intervals and are not a general numerical routine. Blocked use: ['Extending the interval tables to other coefficients without redoing the enclosures', 'Replacing the exact rational enclosures by floating-point evaluation and keeping the certificate']
- **limit-kc-fixed-parameters** (hypothesis): The matrix construction is polynomial only for fixed number of clusters and fixed alphabet exponent, since the dimension grows with the power of the cluster number. Both are fixed before the reduction is applied. Blocked use: ['Treating the construction as polynomial when the alphabet exponent grows with the input', 'Using it with the error parameters allowed to shrink with the formula size']
- **limit-kc-strict-threshold** (formal_scope): Hardness is asserted for loss factors strictly below the threshold, not at the endpoint. The rounding guarantee at the endpoint is quoted in an ideal form without a finite-bit implementation. Blocked use: ['Citing NP-hardness at exactly the threshold loss factor', 'Presenting the endpoint rounding as an implemented finite-precision algorithm']
- **limit-kc-randomized** (formal_scope): The rounding guarantee is in expectation, and the randomized consequence is only that satisfiability would lie in the one-sided randomized class. Hardness for randomized algorithms is not derived from the deterministic assumption alone. Blocked use: ['Claiming that randomized approximation below the threshold is NP-hard outright', 'Dropping the expectation form of the rounding guarantee when quoting it']
- **limit-kc-external-inputs** (formal_scope): The hardness application depends on external theorems: the low-influence bound and the Unique Games reduction. The Gaussian proof supplies only the constant. Blocked use: ['Attributing the hardness result to the Gaussian argument alone', 'Using the application without the companion Unique Games theorem']

Comparator source `GaussianPropeller.lean` (1401 bytes, sha256 `4130e2ee06a20af2578cf886bacc86cc22750a0e17080e69e8675aae25cf608c`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

open MeasureTheory ProbabilityTheory
open scoped ENNReal

namespace GaussianPropeller

abbrev Space (d : ℕ) := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin d)

noncomputable def gaussian (d : ℕ) : Measure (Space d) := stdGaussian (Space d)

def IsPartition {d k : ℕ} (A : Fin k → Set (Space d)) : Prop :=
  (∀ i, MeasurableSet (A i)) ∧ ∀ᵐ x ∂gaussian d, ∃! i, x ∈ A i

noncomputable def centroid {d : ℕ} (A : Set (Space d)) : Space d :=
  ∫ x in A, x ∂gaussian d

noncomputable def value {d k : ℕ} (A : Fin k → Set (Space d)) : ℝ :=
  ∑ i, ‖centroid (A i)‖ ^ 2

def coord {d : ℕ} (j : ℕ) (x : Space d) : ℝ :=
  if h : j < d then x ⟨j, h⟩ else 0

noncomputable def propeller (d k : ℕ) (i : Fin k) : Set (Space d) :=
  if i.val = 0 then {x | |coord 1 x| ≤ Real.sqrt 3 * coord 0 x}
  else if i.val = 1 then {x | 0 ≤ coord 1 x ∧ Real.sqrt 3 * coord 0 x ≤ coord 1 x}
  else if i.val = 2 then {x | coord 1 x ≤ 0 ∧ Real.sqrt 3 * coord 0 x ≤ -coord 1 x}
  else ∅

def AllPartitions : Prop :=
  (∀ (d k : ℕ), 0 < d → 0 < k → ∀ A : Fin k → Set (Space d),
    IsPartition A → value A ≤ 9 / (8 * Real.pi)) ∧
  (∀ (d k : ℕ), 2 ≤ d → 3 ≤ k →
    IsPartition (propeller d k) ∧ value (propeller d k) = 9 / (8 * Real.pi))

theorem all_partitions : AllPartitions := by
  sorry

end GaussianPropeller

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove the Gaussian propeller conjecture: for every finite measurable partition of a Euclidean space, the sum of the squared lengths of its Gaussian first moments is at most $`9/(8\pi)`$. For dimension at least two and at least three cells, three planar sectors of angle $`2\pi/3`$, extended by an orthogonal Euclidean factor, attain the bound.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Let $\gamma_n$ denote the standard Gaussian probability measure on $\mathbb R^n$, with density $(2\pi)^{-n/2}\exp(-\|x\|^2/2)$. For a measurable set $A\subseteq\mathbb R^n$, its Gaussian first moment is $z(A)=\int_A x\,d\gamma_n(x)$. We call this unnormalized vector its *centroid*. Thus a centroid is not divided by the measure of its cell. For positive integers $d,k$ and a measurable partition $\mathcal A=(A_1,\ldots,A_k)$ of $\mathbb R^d$, define $$\mathcal F(\mathcal A)=\sum_{i=1}^k\|z(A_i)\|^2.$$ Partitions are understood up to Gaussian null sets, and empty cells are allowed. The cell probabilities are unrestricted. All norms and inner products below are Euclidean.

The propeller conjecture asks whether three planar sectors meeting at angles $2\pi/3$, extended by an orthogonal Euclidean factor, maximize this functional whenever $d\ge2$ and $k\ge3$. It arose in Khot and Naor’s study of approximate kernel clustering <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[KhotNaor](#ref-KhotNaor)], where Gaussian first moments govern approximation and Unique Games hardness. Their formulation uses $k$ cells in $\mathbb R^{k-1}$, with unused cells permitted. They proved that a maximizer can be chosen to be a conical partition in the span of its centroids and determined the three-cell value <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[KhotNaor](#ref-KhotNaor), Theorem 1.2 and Corollary 3.4].

Heilman, Jagannath, and Naor proved the bound for every finite partition of $\mathbb R^3$ <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[HJN](#ref-HJN), Theorem 1.1]. Their computer-assisted argument treats the remaining four-cell conical configurations through spherical geometry and a finite verification. We use that theorem as an established input. The new estimates below show that an extremizer with five or more active cells cannot have value greater than $9/(8\pi)$. Here an active cell has positive Gaussian measure. This proves the conjecture in all remaining dimensions.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For all positive integers $d$ and $k$, every measurable partition $(A_1,\ldots,A_k)$ of $\mathbb R^d$ satisfies $$\sum_{i=1}^k\left\|\int_{A_i}x\,d\gamma_d(x)\right\|^2
 \le\frac9{8\pi}.$$ Empty cells are allowed. The constant is sharp whenever $d\ge2$ and $k\ge3$: three planar sectors of angle $2\pi/3$, multiplied by the orthogonal complement of their plane, attain equality, with any further cells empty.*

</div>

<a id="sec:example"></a>
#### The attaining propeller

The attaining partition illustrates an important feature of the problem: in every dimension at least two, only three cells are needed once at least three labels are available.

Consider a sector $P$ in $\mathbb R^2$ of opening $2\alpha$, symmetric about the first coordinate axis. Polar integration gives $$\int_P x\,d\gamma_2(x)
 =\frac1{2\pi}\left(\int_0^\infty r^2e^{-r^2/2}\,dr\right)
      \left(\int_{-\alpha}^{\alpha}(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\,d\theta\right)
 =\frac{\sin\alpha}{\sqrt{2\pi}}(1,0).$$ For $\alpha=\pi/3$, three such sectors partition the plane and their squared centroid lengths sum to $3\sin^2(\pi/3)/(2\pi)=9/(8\pi)$. An orthogonal Gaussian factor contributes zero to every centroid, so the construction has the same value in every dimension at least two. The construction proves the attainment assertion in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Figure <a href="#fig:propeller-sectors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:propeller-sectors">1</a> shows the planar partition and its centroid directions.

<figure id="fig:propeller-sectors">

<figcaption>The attaining planar partition consists of three unbounded sectors $S_{1}, S_{2}, S_{3}$ of angle $2π/3$; only a finite window is shown. The bold arrows show the unit centroid directions $ẑ_{i} = z(S_{i})/∥z(S_{i})∥$. Each unnormalized Gaussian centroid has length $\sqrt{3}/(2\sqrt{2\pi})$, so their squared lengths sum to $9/(8π)$. In dimension $d ≥ 2$, use $S_{i} × ℝ^{d − 2}$, with any further cells empty.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Analytic background and related partition problems

The geometric reduction in Khot and Naor’s work is also the starting point here. We reprove its needed form, including attainment, the maximal-score description of the cells, and the effective dimension. The Gaussian-maximum duality used in this reduction is the normalized vector form of their Lemma 3.7. Our estimates then combine the loss on deleting one score with bounds for competition against two residual scores.

The one-score estimate uses the Gaussian Minkowski inequality introduced by Ehrhard for convex sets <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Ehrhard](#ref-Ehrhard)]. Latała extended the inequality when one of the sets is convex <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Latala](#ref-Latala)]; Borell proved the Borel-set version <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Borell](#ref-Borell), Theorem 1.1]. Van Handel later gave a stochastic-game proof <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[vanHandel](#ref-vanHandel)]. We need only the convex case: applied to translates of a cell, the inequality makes its inverse-normal Gaussian measure a concave function of the translation parameter. The translation direction, derivative, and resulting deletion estimate are derived in Section <a href="#sc:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:section">3</a>.

A related question asks which partition maximizes the probability that two correlated Gaussian vectors belong to the same cell, often with equal cell probabilities prescribed. This noise-stability objective is distinct from the unrestricted first-moment functional studied here. Heilman <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[HeilmanHyper](#ref-HeilmanHyper), Theorem 1.7] obtained a conditional route to the propeller bound for three or four cells in arbitrary dimension, assuming an additional stability property of equal-volume noise-stability maximizers. That result retains both the stability hypothesis and the restriction to at most four cells. The present argument addresses all finite cell counts and imposes no cell-mass constraint.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### The kernel-clustering consequence

The Gaussian constant also determines the identity-target kernel-clustering threshold. For a rational symmetric positive semidefinite matrix $A=(a_{pq})_{p,q=1}^N$ with $A\mathbf 1=0$, this problem asks for an assignment $\sigma:[N]\to[k]$ maximizing $$\sum_{p,q=1}^N a_{pq}\mathbf 1_{\{\sigma(p)=\sigma(q)\}}.$$ Here $[N]=\{1,\ldots,N\}$, $\mathbf 1$ is the all-ones vector, and the indicator is one when its subscripts have the same label. Empty clusters are allowed. A loss factor $\alpha$ means returning an assignment worth at least $1/\alpha$ times the maximum value. For fixed $k\ge3$, Khot and Naor’s expected Gaussian rounding guarantee <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[KhotNaor](#ref-KhotNaor), Theorem 2.1] has loss factor $$\alpha_k=\frac{8\pi}{9}\left(1-\frac1k\right).$$ Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> evaluates the Gaussian partition parameter in their hardness analysis. Together with their low-influence theorem and the companion paper *The Unique Games Theorem <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[UniqueGamesTheorem](#ref-UniqueGamesTheorem), Theorem 1.1]* , it gives NP-hardness of every deterministic approximation with a fixed loss factor below $\alpha_k$. Section <a href="#kc:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kc:section">6</a> proves this application on rational inputs and specifies the ideal rounding convention at the endpoint. The separate Unique Games input is needed only for the hardness statement.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Proof strategy

The difficulty is to control all measurable partitions while allowing both their shapes and their probabilities to vary. We first replace this infinite-dimensional optimization by a finite list of Gaussian linear scores. Two different consequences of optimality then constrain that list: deleting one score costs a definite amount of expected maximum, and competing against two scores constrains a covariance determinant.

Section <a href="#ext:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:section">2</a> proves the reduction in full. Norm duality identifies the square root of the largest possible partition value with the largest possible expectation of the maximum score over lists of vectors with total squared norm one. Compactness gives an extremizer. Among extremizers, choose one with the fewest cells of positive measure. Merging two cells shows that its nonzero centroids $z_1,\ldots,z_m$ have strictly negative pairwise inner products. Its cells agree almost everywhere with the cones where the corresponding score $\langle z_i,x\rangle$ is largest. The centroids span a space of dimension $m-1$. The theorem of Heilman, Jagannath, and Naor therefore excludes $m\le4$ from any counterexample to the desired bound.

For the remaining case $m\ge5$, let $C$ be the optimal value, let $r_i=\|z_i\|/\sqrt C$ be the normalized centroid lengths, and let $P_i$ be the cell probabilities. Then $\sum_i r_i^2=\sum_iP_i=1$. Section <a href="#sc:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:section">3</a> bounds each $P_i$ below in terms of $r_i$. Halfspace rearrangement gives one such estimate; when $m=5$, spherical rearrangement in the four-dimensional centroid span improves it. A further bound comes from removing one score and recentering the remaining vectors. The extremal score inequality gives a lower bound for the loss. Translations of the deleted score’s winning cone, together with Ehrhard’s inequality, give an upper bound. Comparing the two produces a scalar constraint on $P_i$ and $r_i$.

Section <a href="#geo:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:section">4</a> uses the cell with the smallest normalized centroid length. Projecting the other scores orthogonally to its centroid gives residual Gaussian scores independent of the score in that direction. Their integrals over the chosen cell vanish. For two residual scores, these identities allow us to estimate a positive-part integral by replacing the joint density with its maximum. The denominator of this two-dimensional Gaussian density bound is the square root of the residual covariance determinant. The resulting pairwise bound yields two inequalities involving the three largest normalized centroid lengths and the smallest one.

Finally, Section <a href="#elim:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="elim:section">5</a> combines the scalar probability bounds with the identity $\sum_iP_i=1$. The available probability mass forces the third-largest squared length to be too large relative to the smallest length, contradicting the determinant bound. This excludes an optimal value above $9/(8\pi)$. Appendix <a href="#cert:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:arithmetic">7</a> gives exact rational enclosures for every finite numerical comparison, including the complete interval cover needed for the scalar constraint. The geometric proof uses the three-dimensional propeller bound and Ehrhard’s inequality as its external theorem inputs; the remaining geometric estimates are proved here. Section <a href="#kc:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kc:section">6</a> gives a separate kernel-clustering application. Its reduction adapts Khot and Naor’s construction and uses two additional theorem inputs: their discrete Fourier estimate and the separate Unique Games theorem in <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[UniqueGamesTheorem](#ref-UniqueGamesTheorem)].

<a id="ext:section"></a>
### Extremal partitions and their centroids

Our goal is to replace an arbitrary measurable partition by an optimal partition described by finitely many Gaussian linear scores. We include the extremal reduction of Khot and Naor <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[KhotNaor](#ref-KhotNaor), Theorem 1.2 and Lemma 3.3] in the form needed below.

It suffices to prove the upper bound for $n+1$ cells in $\mathbb R^n$ with $n\ge4$. Indeed, given $k$ cells in $\mathbb R^d$, put $n=\max\{d,k-1,4\}$, take their products with $\mathbb R^{n-d}$, and append $n+1-k$ empty cells. Gaussian product decomposition appends zero coordinates to each first moment and preserves the objective.

Fix such an $n$ and put $N=n+1$. All partitions below are measurable partitions up to Gaussian null sets, and empty cells are permitted. Write $$C=\sup_{(A_1,\ldots,A_N)}
       \sum_{i=1}^N\left\|\int_{A_i}x\,d\gamma_n(x)\right\|^2,
 \qquad B=\frac{3}{2\sqrt{2\pi}}.$$ The moments exist, and Cauchy–Schwarz on each cell gives $C\le n$. We first record the normalized vector form of the Gaussian-maximum description in <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[KhotNaor](#ref-KhotNaor), Lemma 3.7]. Its proof also gives attainment directly.

<a id="stmt-ext-duality"></a>
<div id="ext:duality" class="lemma">

<a id="ext:dual-formula"></a>
<a id="ext:shorter-list"></a>
**Lemma 2**. *If $g$ is a standard Gaussian vector in $\mathbb R^n$, then $$\label{ext:dual-formula}
 \sqrt C=
 \max_{\sum_{i=1}^N\|v_i\|^2=1}
       \mathbb E\max_{1\le i\le N}\langle v_i,g\rangle.$$ The supremum defining $C$ is attained. Moreover, for every $1\le k\le N$ and every list $v_1,\ldots,v_k\in\mathbb R^n$, $$\label{ext:shorter-list}
 \mathbb E\max_{1\le i\le k}\langle v_i,g\rangle
 \le \sqrt C\left(\sum_{i=1}^k\|v_i\|^2\right)^{1/2}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ext-duality"><summary>Proof of stmt-ext-duality</summary>

*Proof.* For a partition with moments $z_i=\int_{A_i}x\,d\gamma_n(x)$, Euclidean norm duality gives $$\left(\sum_i\|z_i\|^2\right)^{1/2}
 =\max_{\sum_i\|v_i\|^2=1}
       \sum_i\int_{A_i}\langle v_i,x\rangle\,d\gamma_n(x).$$ Taking the supremum jointly over partitions and lists, and then first over partitions, yields the right side of <a href="#ext:dual-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:dual-formula">[ext:dual-formula]</a>. Indeed, for each fixed list the pointwise maximum is attained by assigning $x$ to a largest score $\langle v_i,x\rangle$, with ties resolved by the smallest index. This gives a measurable partition. The expected maximum is continuous in the list: the absolute difference of two maxima is at most $\|g\|\max_i\|v_i-w_i\|$. Compactness of the sphere of lists therefore gives a maximizing list. Its score partition has moment norm at least its pairing with that list, namely $\sqrt C$, so it attains $C$.

For <a href="#ext:shorter-list" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:shorter-list">[ext:shorter-list]</a>, let $Q_1,\ldots,Q_k$ be a score partition for the specified list, and put $b_i=\int_{Q_i}x\,d\gamma_n(x)$. Appending $N-k$ empty cells shows that $\sum_i\|b_i\|^2\le C$. Consequently $$\mathbb E\max_i\langle v_i,g\rangle
 =\sum_i\langle v_i,b_i\rangle
 \le\left(\sum_i\|v_i\|^2\right)^{1/2}
      \left(\sum_i\|b_i\|^2\right)^{1/2},$$ which proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="ext:contradiction"></a>
<a id="ext:centroid-identities"></a>
We argue by contradiction and henceforth assume $$\label{ext:contradiction}
 C>B^2=\frac{9}{8\pi}.$$ Choose an optimal partition with the fewest cells of positive measure, discard its null cells, and call the remaining cells *active*. Denote their moments by $z_1,\ldots,z_m$. Thus $$\label{ext:centroid-identities}
 \sum_{i=1}^m z_i=0,
 \qquad \sum_{i=1}^m\|z_i\|^2=C.$$

The following structure statement develops the minimal-cell reduction of <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[KhotNaor](#ref-KhotNaor), Lemma 3.3]; the rank assertions are proved directly from the Gram matrix.

<a id="stmt-ext-structure"></a>
<div id="ext:structure" class="lemma">

<a id="ext:cones"></a>
**Lemma 3**. *The centroids satisfy $\langle z_i,z_j\rangle<0$ for $i\ne j$. Their span $V$ has dimension $m-1$, every proper subset of the centroids is linearly independent, and, for each fixed $i$, the $m-1$ vectors $z_i-z_j$, $j\ne i$, form a basis of $V$. The optimal cells have the closed conical representatives $$\label{ext:cones}
 K_i=\{x\in\mathbb R^n:
          \langle z_i-z_j,x\rangle\ge0\text{ for every }j\ne i\}.$$ Furthermore, $m\ge5$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ext-structure"><summary>Proof of stmt-ext-structure</summary>

*Proof.* Since $C>0$, we have $m\ge2$. Merging two cells changes the objective by $2\langle z_i,z_j\rangle$. A positive product would contradict optimality; a zero product would contradict minimality of $m$. Thus every such product is negative, and in particular every $z_i$ is nonzero.

For the original optimal partition, pairing each cell with its own centroid gives $$C\le\mathbb E\max_i\langle z_i,g\rangle\le C,$$ where the second inequality is <a href="#ext:shorter-list" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:shorter-list">[ext:shorter-list]</a>. The difference between the maximum score and the assigned score is nonnegative and has zero integral, so it vanishes almost everywhere. The centroids are distinct, and their score ties lie on finitely many Gaussian-null hyperplanes. This proves <a href="#ext:cones" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:cones">[ext:cones]</a>; overlaps between these closed representatives are immaterial.

Let $Q=(\langle z_i,z_j\rangle)_{i,j=1}^m$ be the Gram matrix. Its row sums vanish by <a href="#ext:centroid-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:centroid-identities">[ext:centroid-identities]</a>. Hence, for every $a=(a_1,\ldots,a_m)\in\mathbb R^m$, <a id="ext:laplacian"></a>$$\label{ext:laplacian}
 a^{\mathsf T}Qa
 =\sum_{i<j}(-\langle z_i,z_j\rangle)(a_i-a_j)^2.$$ All weights are positive, so the kernel of $Q$ consists exactly of constant vectors. Its rank is $m-1$, and the only linear relations among the centroids are multiples of their sum. A dependence on a proper subset would have a zero coefficient and therefore must be trivial. A relation among $z_i-z_j$, $j\ne i$, gives a relation among all centroids whose coefficients sum to zero; it too must be trivial. This proves the linear-algebra assertions.

Each $K_i$ depends only on the orthogonal projection onto $V$. If $d=\dim V$, then orthogonal Gaussian product decomposition identifies $K_i$ with $(K_i\cap V)\times V^\perp$, and its moment is the moment of $K_i\cap V$ in $V$, extended by zero on $V^\perp$. If $m\le4$, then $d\le3$. Taking the product of this partition of $V\cong\mathbb R^d$ with $\mathbb R^{3-d}$ preserves its moment norms. The three-dimensional propeller theorem of Heilman, Jagannath, and Naor <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[HJN](#ref-HJN), Theorem 1.1], which permits any finite number of cells, would then give $C\le9/(8\pi)$, contradicting <a href="#ext:contradiction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:contradiction">[ext:contradiction]</a>. Thus $m\ge5$. ◻

</details>

We have reduced a hypothetical counterexample to at least five active cells whose centroid span has dimension one less than their number. From now on we work in $V\cong\mathbb R^{m-1}$, use $K_i$ for the reduced cones, and let $g$ be standard Gaussian in this space. Their moments remain exactly $z_i$. Inequality <a href="#ext:shorter-list" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:shorter-list">[ext:shorter-list]</a> remains valid for lists in $V$, by embedding them in $\mathbb R^n$. Set <a id="ext:notation"></a>$$\label{ext:notation}
 X_i=\langle z_i,g\rangle,
 \qquad P_i=\gamma_{m-1}(K_i),
 \qquad r_i=\frac{\|z_i\|}{\sqrt C}.$$ Then $P_i>0$, $r_i>0$, $\sum_iP_i=\sum_i r_i^2=1$, and $\mathbb E\max_iX_i=C$. Since $\sum_iX_i=0$, a winning score is nonnegative. Thus $K_i\subseteq\{X_i\ge0\}$ and <a id="ext:half-probability"></a>$$\label{ext:half-probability}
 P_i\le\frac12\qquad(1\le i\le m).$$

<a id="sc:section"></a>
### Probability bounds for the cells

Throughout this section, we work with the putative extremizer above: $C>B^2$, $m\ge5$, and $B=3/(2\sqrt{2\pi})$. We will obtain lower bounds for each cell probability in terms of its normalized centroid length. Since the probabilities sum to one, these bounds constrain how the normalized centroid lengths can be distributed among the cells. Every terminating decimal in the proof denotes an exact rational number. Write $$\phi(x)=\frac{e^{-x^2/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}},\qquad
 \Phi(x)=\int_{-\infty}^x\phi(u)\,du,\qquad
 r_i=\frac{|z_i|}{\sqrt C},\qquad P_i=\gamma_{m-1}(K_i).$$ The winning cone $K_i$ lies in $\{x:\langle z_i,x\rangle\ge0\}$, because the scores sum to zero. Thus $0<P_i\le1/2$.

<a id="stmt-sc-bounds"></a>
<div id="sc:bounds" class="lemma">

<a id="sc:three-bounds"></a>
**Lemma 4**. *Set $$A=\begin{cases}0.929,&m=5,\\0.884,&m\ge6.\end{cases}$$ For every $i$, $$\label{sc:three-bounds}
 r_i\le\frac23,\qquad P_i\ge Ar_i^2,\qquad
 P_i\ge0.415r_i+0.15r_i^2.$$*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Rearrangement bounds

<a id="sc:halfspace-ratio"></a>
Fix a cell, and temporarily suppress its index. Let $e=z/|z|$, $x=\Phi^{-1}(1-P)$, and $H=\{g:\langle e,g\rangle\ge x\}$. Since $\gamma_{m-1}(H)=P$, $$|z|-\int_H\langle e,g\rangle\,d\gamma_{m-1}(g)
 =\int(\langle e,g\rangle-x)(1_K-1_H)\,d\gamma_{m-1}(g)\le0.$$ Consequently $|z|\le\phi(x)$. As $\sqrt C>B=(3/2)\phi(0)$, this proves $r<2/3$ and $$\label{sc:halfspace-ratio}
 \frac P{r^2}\ge \frac94 e^{x^2}\Phi(-x).$$ For completeness, the minimum on the right can be localized by a one-dimensional argument. Put $M(x)=\phi(x)/\Phi(-x)$. The logarithmic derivative of $f(x)=(9/4)e^{x^2}\Phi(-x)$ is $2x-M(x)$, and $$M'(x)=1-\operatorname{Var}(Z\mid Z>x)<1$$ for a standard normal variable $Z$. Hence $(\log f)'$ is strictly increasing. Its signs at $0.6119$ and $0.6121$ are opposite, so $f$ has a unique global minimum in that interval. The elementary bounds in Appendix <a href="#cert:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:arithmetic">7</a> give <a id="sc:halfspace-minimum"></a>$$\label{sc:halfspace-minimum}
 \min_{x\in\mathbb R}f(x)>0.8843837>0.884.$$ This proves the required quadratic estimate when $m\ge6$.

<a id="sc:cap-ratio"></a>
When $m=5$, conicality improves the estimate. Work in the four-dimensional span of the centroids and write the standard Gaussian as $RU$, where $U$ is uniform on $S^3$ and independent of $R$. The coordinate $\langle e,U\rangle$ has density $(2/\pi)\sqrt{1-u^2}$ on $[-1,1]$. The same threshold comparison as above shows that, among spherical sets of measure $P$, an upper cap maximizes the first moment in direction $e$. Since $P\le1/2$, write its threshold as $x\in[0,1)$. Its probability is $$p(x)=\frac12-\frac{\arcsin x+x\sqrt{1-x^2}}\pi.$$ Using $\mathbb ER=3\sqrt{2\pi}/4$, its Gaussian first moment is $$\mathbb ER\,\frac2\pi\int_x^1 u\sqrt{1-u^2}\,du
 =\phi(0)(1-x^2)^{3/2}.$$ It follows that $$\label{sc:cap-ratio}
 \frac P{r^2}\ge R(x):=\frac94\frac{p(x)}{(1-x^2)^3}.$$ To identify the global minimum, define $$F(x)=6xp(x)-\frac2\pi(1-x^2)^{3/2},\qquad
 H(x)=p(x)-\frac x\pi\sqrt{1-x^2}.$$ Direct differentiation gives $$R'(x)=\frac94\frac{F(x)}{(1-x^2)^4},\qquad
 F'(x)=6H(x),\qquad
 H'(x)=\frac{4x^2-3}{\pi\sqrt{1-x^2}}.$$ Thus $H$ first decreases and then increases. Since $H(0)=1/2$ and $H(1)=0$, it has exactly one zero in $(0,1)$; it is negative after that zero. Accordingly $F$ first increases and then decreases to $F(1)=0$. The certified signs $F(0.29)<0<F(0.294)$ show that $F$ has exactly one interior zero and that this zero lies in $(0.29,0.294)$. It is the unique minimizer of $R$. Appendix <a href="#cert:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:arithmetic">7</a> gives $R(0.29)>0.9312526$ and $|R'|<0.03$ on this interval, whence <a id="sc:cap-minimum"></a>$$\label{sc:cap-minimum}
 \min_{0\le x<1}R(x)>0.9311326>0.929.$$ The first two assertions of Lemma <a href="#sc:bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:bounds">4</a> follow.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### The loss on removing one score

<a id="sc:loss-lower"></a>
Rearrangement has supplied the radius bound and the quadratic probability bound. To obtain the remaining linear–quadratic estimate in Lemma <a href="#sc:bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:bounds">4</a>, we use the behavior of the winning probability under translation. Fix $i$, put $X_j=\langle z_j,g\rangle$, and set $\alpha=m/(m-1)$. Removing $z_i$ and recentering the remaining vectors produces $z_j+z_i/(m-1)$, $j\ne i$, whose squared norms sum to $C-\alpha|z_i|^2$. A common translation of the scores has zero expectation. The extremal dual bound therefore gives $$\mathbb E\max_{j\ne i}X_j\le
 \sqrt C\sqrt{C-\alpha|z_i|^2}.$$ Since $\mathbb E\max_jX_j=C$, the loss satisfies $$\label{sc:loss-lower}
 \mathbb E\bigl(X_i-\max_{j\ne i}X_j\bigr)_+
 \ge C\bigl(1-\sqrt{1-\alpha r_i^2}\bigr).$$ Here $u_+=\max(u,0)$.

<a id="sc:translation-derivative"></a>
The vectors $z_i-z_j$, $j\ne i$, form a basis of the centroid span. There is therefore a vector $h$ in that span satisfying $\langle h,z_i-z_j\rangle=1$ for every $j\ne i$. For $u\ge0$, the gap probability is $$p_i(u):=\Pr\{X_i\ge X_j+u\text{ for all }j\ne i\}
       =\gamma_{m-1}(K_i+uh).$$ Summing the equations defining $h$ gives $\langle h,z_i\rangle=(m-1)/m=1/\alpha$. Differentiation of the translated Gaussian density, justified by Gaussian integrability, yields $$\label{sc:translation-derivative}
 p_i'(0)=-\int_{K_i}\langle h,x\rangle\,d\gamma_{m-1}(x)
        =-\langle h,z_i\rangle=-\frac1\alpha.$$

<a id="sc:loss-upper"></a>
<a id="sc:loss-constraint"></a>
Ehrhard’s inequality states that $\Phi^{-1}\circ\gamma_{m-1}$ is concave under Minkowski interpolation of convex sets <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Ehrhard](#ref-Ehrhard)]; we use the formulation in <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Borell](#ref-Borell), Theorem 1.1], which also allows general Borel sets. The cone $K_i$ is closed and convex. Its score-difference normals form a basis, so it has nonempty interior and is a proper subset of the centroid span. Minkowski interpolation of two translates of $K_i$ is the translate with the interpolated displacement. Hence $u\mapsto\Phi^{-1}(p_i(u))$ is concave. All finite translates have measure strictly between zero and one, and the derivative at zero exists by <a href="#sc:translation-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:translation-derivative">[sc:translation-derivative]</a>. With $q=\Phi^{-1}(P_i)$, the supporting tangent gives $$p_i(u)\le\Phi\!\left(q-\frac{u}{\alpha\phi(q)}\right)
 \qquad(u\ge0).$$ Integration, using $(q\Phi(q)+\phi(q))'=\Phi(q)$, gives $$\label{sc:loss-upper}
 \mathbb E\bigl(X_i-\max_{j\ne i}X_j\bigr)_+
 =\int_0^\infty p_i(u)\,du
 \le\alpha\phi(q)\bigl(q\Phi(q)+\phi(q)\bigr).$$ Define $$G(q)=\frac{\phi(q)\bigl(q\Phi(q)+\phi(q)\bigr)}{\Phi(q)^2}.$$ Combining <a href="#sc:loss-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:loss-lower">[sc:loss-lower]</a> and <a href="#sc:loss-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:loss-upper">[sc:loss-upper]</a>, rationalizing, and canceling $\alpha$, we obtain $$\label{sc:loss-constraint}
 P_i^2G\bigl(\Phi^{-1}(P_i)\bigr)
 \ge\frac{Cr_i^2}{1+\sqrt{1-\alpha r_i^2}}
 \ge\frac{B^2r_i^2}{1+\sqrt{1-r_i^2}}.$$

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### A linear–quadratic probability bound

The loss constraint is implicit in the cell probability. To extract an explicit lower bound, we first prove two monotonicity properties of $G$.

<a id="stmt-sc-truncated-variance"></a>
<div id="sc:truncated-variance" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5**. *The function $G$ is nonincreasing and satisfies $0<G(q)<1$. Moreover, $$P\longmapsto P^2G\bigl(\Phi^{-1}(P)\bigr)$$ is increasing on $(0,1/2]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sc-truncated-variance"><summary>Proof of stmt-sc-truncated-variance</summary>

*Proof.* Put $\lambda=\phi(q)/\Phi(q)$. Integration of the normal density gives $$\mathbb E(Z\mid Z\le q)=-\lambda,\qquad
 V(q):=\operatorname{Var}(Z\mid Z\le q)=1-q\lambda-\lambda^2.$$ Thus $G=1-V=\lambda(q+\lambda)$. The distance $U=q-Z$, under this conditioning, has positive mean $q+\lambda$ and positive variance, proving $0<G<1$. Its survival function is $S(u)=\Phi(q-u)/\Phi(q)$, $u\ge0$. The identity $(\log\Phi)''(q)=-\lambda(q+\lambda)<0$ shows that $\log S$ is concave. Its increments therefore satisfy $$\log S(u+v)-\log S(u)\le\log S(v)-\log S(0).$$ Since $S(0)=1$, this gives $S(u+v)\le S(u)S(v)$. Integrating over $u,v\ge0$ gives $$\frac12\mathbb EU^2
 =\int_0^\infty\!\int_0^\infty S(u+v)\,du\,dv
 \le(\mathbb EU)^2.$$ Hence $V\le(q+\lambda)^2$. Direct differentiation now gives $$V'(q)=\lambda\bigl((q+\lambda)^2-V(q)\bigr)\ge0,$$ so $G$ is nonincreasing. Finally, for $q\le0$, both positive factors of $\phi(q)(q\Phi(q)+\phi(q))$ are increasing in $q$, proving the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="sc:test-probability"></a>
We finish the proof of Lemma <a href="#sc:bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:bounds">4</a>. Set $P_0(r)=(0.415+0.15r)r$ for $0<r\le2/3$. Then $P_0(r)\le P_0(2/3)=103/300<1/2$. We will prove $$\label{sc:test-probability}
 (0.415+0.15r)^2G\bigl(\Phi^{-1}(P_0(r))\bigr)
 <\frac{B^2}{1+\sqrt{1-r^2}}.$$ By Lemma <a href="#sc:truncated-variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:truncated-variance">5</a> and <a href="#sc:loss-constraint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:loss-constraint">[sc:loss-constraint]</a>, this implies $P_i>P_0(r_i)$, which is stronger than the last assertion of Lemma <a href="#sc:bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:bounds">4</a>.

Here is a finite interval verification of <a href="#sc:test-probability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:test-probability">[sc:test-probability]</a>. In each row of Table <a href="#sc:interval-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:interval-table">1</a>, $U$ bounds $G(\Phi^{-1}(P_0(r)))$ above throughout $[a,b]\cap(0,2/3]$. The first row uses $G<1$; the other rows follow from monotonicity and the certified quantile bounds in Appendix <a href="#cert:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:arithmetic">7</a>. The last column is a strict lower bound for $$\Delta(a,b,U):=
 \frac{B^2}{1+\sqrt{1-a^2}}-U(0.415+0.15b)^2.$$ It is positive in every row. The coefficient $(0.415+0.15r)^2$ and the right side of <a href="#sc:test-probability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:test-probability">[sc:test-probability]</a> are increasing in $r$, so these endpoint inequalities cover every point of the intervals, not only their endpoints.

<div id="sc:interval-table">

| $a$  |  $b$  |  $U$  | $10^6\Delta(a,b,U)>$ |
|:----:|:-----:|:-----:|:--------------------:|
|  0   | 0.05  |   1   |         542          |
| 0.05 | 0.14  | 0.91  |         6173         |
| 0.14 | 0.24  | 0.87  |         2976         |
| 0.24 | 0.33  | 0.836 |         1328         |
| 0.33 | 0.41  | 0.809 |         523          |
| 0.41 | 0.48  | 0.788 |         392          |
| 0.48 | 0.54  | 0.771 |         1076         |
| 0.54 | 0.59  | 0.756 |         2787         |
| 0.59 | 0.64  | 0.743 |         4115         |
| 0.64 | $2/3$ | 0.73  |         8886         |

Bounds proving <a href="#sc:test-probability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:test-probability">[sc:test-probability]</a>. All displayed finite decimals are exact rational numbers.

</div>

<a id="geo:section"></a>
### Competition with two other cells

Continue with the minimal-active-cell optimal partition from Lemma <a href="#ext:structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:structure">3</a>, satisfying $C>B^2$ and $m\ge5$. The scalar estimates constrain one cell at a time. We now use the first-moment identity for the cell with smallest centroid norm to obtain constraints on pairs of the other centroids. Relabel the nonzero centroids so that $$r_1\ge r_2\ge\cdots\ge r_m=t>0,
 \qquad
 L=r_1^2+r_2^2,
 \qquad c=r_3^2.$$ Thus the three largest radii and the chosen smallest radius have distinct indices, including when some radii are equal. We will use the centroid identity for this cell to control the joint distribution of any two competing scores.

<a id="geo:coefficients"></a>
<a id="geo:decomposition"></a>
<a id="geo:covariance"></a>
Let $g$ be a standard Gaussian vector in the ambient Euclidean space, and put $$e=\frac{z_m}{|z_m|},\qquad T=\langle e,g\rangle,
 \qquad
 a_j=-\frac{\langle z_m,z_j\rangle}{|z_m|^2}
 \quad(1\le j<m).$$ Strict negativity of the off-diagonal products and $\sum_jz_j=0$ imply $$a_j>0,\qquad \sum_{j<m}a_j=1.
 \label{geo:coefficients}$$ Define the residual vectors and scores by $$w_j=\frac{z_j}{\sqrt C}+a_jte,
 \qquad Y_j=\langle w_j,g\rangle.$$ Each $w_j$ is orthogonal to $e$. Consequently $Y=(Y_j)_{j<m}$ is a centered Gaussian vector independent of the standard normal variable $T$, and $$\frac{\langle z_j,g\rangle}{\sqrt C}=-a_jtT+Y_j.
 \label{geo:decomposition}$$ Its covariance matrix $H$ has entries $$H_{jk}=\frac{\langle z_j,z_k\rangle}{C}-a_ja_kt^2,
 \qquad h_j:=H_{jj}=r_j^2-a_j^2t^2.
 \label{geo:covariance}$$ Since $\sum_{j<m}w_j=0$, every row of $H$ sums to zero; also $H_{jk}<0$ for $j\ne k$. For distinct $j,k<m$, set $$D_{jk}=\sqrt{h_jh_k-H_{jk}^2}.$$ These quantities are positive. Indeed, a linear dependence between $w_j$ and $w_k$ would give a dependence between $z_j,z_k,z_m$, whereas every proper subset of the centroids is linearly independent and $m\ge5$. In particular, all $h_j$ are positive.

<a id="stmt-geo-pair"></a>
<div id="geo:pair" class="lemma">

<a id="geo:pair-bound"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Two competing scores). *For every pair of distinct indices $j,k<m$, $$D_{jk}\le \frac3\pi\,t(1+a_j)(1+a_k).
 \label{geo:pair-bound}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-pair"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-pair</summary>

<a id="geo:centroid-identities"></a>
<a id="geo:density-bound"></a>
*Proof.* Let $K$ be the winning cone for the score $\langle z_m,g\rangle$. The partition agrees with its score cones almost everywhere, so $\mathbb E[\mathbf 1_Kg]=z_m$. The definitions of $T$ and $Y_j$ give $$\mathbb E[\mathbf 1_KT]=|z_m|=\sqrt C\,t>Bt,
 \qquad
 \mathbb E[\mathbf 1_KY_j]=\langle w_j,z_m\rangle=0.
 \label{geo:centroid-identities}$$ The scores sum to zero. Their maximum is therefore nonnegative, and on $K$ we have, by <a href="#geo:decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:decomposition">[geo:decomposition]</a>, $$T\ge0,\qquad Y_j\le(1+a_j)tT\quad(j<m).$$ Fix distinct $j,k<m$ and write $$U=\frac{Y_j}{(1+a_j)t},\qquad
 V=\frac{Y_k}{(1+a_k)t}.$$ The pair $(U,V)$ is independent of $T$, and its Gaussian density is bounded above everywhere by $$M=\frac{t^2(1+a_j)(1+a_k)}{2\pi D_{jk}}.
 \label{geo:density-bound}$$ Here the denominator follows by taking the square root of the covariance determinant after the two rescalings.

For a real number $x$, write $x_+=\max\{x,0\}$. Adding the two zero expectations from <a href="#geo:centroid-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:centroid-identities">[geo:centroid-identities]</a>, taking a positive part, and then enlarging the integration region yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E[\mathbf 1_KT]
 &=\mathbb E[\mathbf 1_K(T+U+V)]\\
 &\le\mathbb E\big[
 \mathbf 1_{\{T\ge0,\ U\le T,\ V\le T\}}(T+U+V)_+\big].
\end{aligned}$$ For a fixed $s\ge0$, within $u\le s$, $v\le s$ the positive integrand is supported on the triangle with vertices $(s,s)$, $(s,-2s)$, and $(-2s,s)$. The corresponding unweighted planar integral is $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{u\le s}\int_{v\le s}(s+u+v)_+\,dv\,du
 &=\int_0^\infty\int_0^\infty(3s-a-b)_+\,db\,da\\
 &=\int_0^{3s}\int_0^{3s-a}(3s-a-b)\,db\,da
 =\frac{(3s)^3}{6},
\end{aligned}$$ where $a=s-u$ and $b=s-v$. Figure <a href="#fig:residual-score-triangle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:residual-score-triangle">2</a> shows this integration region and its affine integrand.

<figure id="fig:residual-score-triangle">
<p>$$\int_{\Delta_s}h_s(u,v)\,du\,dv
    =\underbrace{\frac{(3s)^2}{2}}_{\text{area}}
      \underbrace{\frac{3s+0+0}{3}}_{\text{mean affine height}}
    =\frac{27s^3}{6}.$$</p>
<figcaption>For fixed $T = s > 0$, the positive integrand is supported, within $u ≤ s$ and $v ≤ s$, on $Δ_{s} = {(u,v) : u ≤ s, v ≤ s, s + u + v ≥ 0}$. Here $h_{s}(u,v) = s + u + v$ is an affine height, with the displayed values at the vertices and barycenter. The shading marks the integration region, not the support or density of the Gaussian pair $(U,V)$. Multiplying the unweighted integral by the density bound $M$ gives the estimate used in the proof. At $s = 0$ the triangle degenerates and the integral is zero.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-residual-score-triangle"></a>

                      font=\small]

    \draw[gray!70,->] (-2.66,0) -- (1.80,0) node[right] {$u$};
    \draw[gray!70,->] (0,-2.62) -- (0,1.76) node[above] {$v$};
    \draw[thick] (-2,1) -- (1,1) -- (1,-2) -- cycle;

      {$(-2s,s)$\\[-1pt] $h_s=0$};

    \node[right=3pt] at (1,-.80) {$u=s$};
    \node[rotate=-45,below=4pt] at (-1.02,.02) {$u+v=-s$};
    \node at (.52,.50) {$\Delta_s$};
