Visual witness description of PDF page 12 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A Cartesian coordinate diagram showing the region $\Delta_s$ in the $(u, v)$ plane. The horizontal axis is labeled $u$ and the vertical axis is labeled $v$. The region $\Delta_s$ is a right isosceles triangle shaded in purple/blue. Its boundaries are the horizontal line $v = s$, the vertical line $u = s$, and the hypotenuse along the line labeled $u + v = -s$. The vertices are labeled $(-2s, s)$ with $h_s = 0$ (top-left), $(s, s)$ with $h_s = 3s$ (top-right), and $(s, -2s)$ with $h_s = 0$ (bottom-right). The origin is marked with a point $(0, 0)$ inside the triangle with label $h_s = s$. Below the diagram, the integral formula is displayed: $\int_{\Delta_s} h_s(u, v) \, du \, dv = \underbrace{\frac{(3s)^2}{2}}_{\text{area}} \underbrace{\frac{3s + 0 + 0}{3}}_{\text{mean affine height}} = \frac{27s^3}{6}$.
```


Using <a href="#geo:density-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:density-bound">[geo:density-bound]</a>, independence, and $\mathbb E[T_+^3]=2/\sqrt{2\pi}$, we obtain $$Bt<\mathbb E[\mathbf 1_KT]
 \le \frac{27M}{6}\,\mathbb E[T_+^3]
 =\frac{9t^2(1+a_j)(1+a_k)}{2\pi\sqrt{2\pi}\,D_{jk}}.$$ Since $B=3/(2\sqrt{2\pi})$, this gives <a href="#geo:pair-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:pair-bound">[geo:pair-bound]</a>, in fact with a strict inequality. ◻

</details>

The pairwise inequality becomes useful for the partition problem when we sum it over competitors with the largest centroid lengths. The negative off-diagonal covariances prevent all of these pairwise determinants from being small simultaneously.

<a id="stmt-geo-constraints"></a>
<div id="geo:constraints" class="corollary">

<a id="geo:first-constraint"></a>
<a id="geo:second-constraint"></a>
**Corollary 7** (Constraints on the three largest radii). *The quantities $L,c,t$ satisfy $$\begin{aligned}
 (c-t^2)(L-c-2t^2)
 &\le8\left(\frac3\pi\right)^2t^2,
 \label{geo:first-constraint}\\
 \frac32(c-t^2)^2
 &\le10\left(\frac3\pi\right)^2t^2.
 \label{geo:second-constraint}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-constraints"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-constraints</summary>

<a id="geo:row-lower"></a>
*Proof.* For the first inequality, the signs and row sums of $H$ give $$H_{31}^2+H_{32}^2
 \le (|H_{31}|+|H_{32}|)^2\le h_3^2.$$ It follows that $$D_{31}^2+D_{32}^2\ge h_3(h_1+h_2-h_3).
 \label{geo:row-lower}$$ By <a href="#geo:covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:covariance">[geo:covariance]</a> and $0<a_j\le1$, $$h_3\ge c-t^2\ge0,
 \qquad h_1+h_2-h_3\ge L-c-2t^2.$$ If $L-c-2t^2\ge0$, these inequalities and <a href="#geo:row-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:row-lower">[geo:row-lower]</a> show that $$D_{31}^2+D_{32}^2\ge(c-t^2)(L-c-2t^2).$$ If $L-c-2t^2<0$, the same conclusion follows from the nonnegativity of the determinant squares.

On the other hand, Lemma <a href="#geo:pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:pair">6</a> gives $$D_{31}^2+D_{32}^2
 \le\left(\frac3\pi\right)^2t^2
 (1+a_3)^2\big((1+a_1)^2+(1+a_2)^2\big).$$ Because $a_1+a_2\le1-a_3$, $$(1+a_1)^2+(1+a_2)^2
 \le (2-a_3)^2+1.$$ For $0\le x\le1$, the identity $$8-(1+x)^2\big((2-x)^2+1\big)=(1-x)^2(3-x^2)\ge0$$ therefore proves <a href="#geo:first-constraint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:first-constraint">[geo:first-constraint]</a>.

<a id="geo:correlation-lower"></a>
For the second inequality, let $\rho_{jk}=H_{jk}/\sqrt{h_jh_k}$ for $1\le j<k\le3$. These correlations belong to $[-1,0]$. Since $$0\le\operatorname{Var}\left(\sum_{j=1}^3\frac{Y_j}{\sqrt{h_j}}\right)
 =3+2\sum_{1\le j<k\le3}\rho_{jk},$$ we have $$\sum_{j<k\le3}\rho_{jk}^2
 \le\sum_{j<k\le3}|\rho_{jk}|\le\frac32.$$ Each $h_j\ge c-t^2\ge0$ for $j\le3$. Thus $$\sum_{j<k\le3}D_{jk}^2
 =\sum_{j<k\le3}h_jh_k(1-\rho_{jk}^2)
 \ge\frac32(c-t^2)^2.
 \label{geo:correlation-lower}$$ To bound the sum furnished by Lemma <a href="#geo:pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:pair">6</a>, put $$s_1=a_1+a_2+a_3,\qquad
 s_2=a_1a_2+a_1a_3+a_2a_3,\qquad s_3=a_1a_2a_3.$$ Equation <a href="#geo:coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:coefficients">[geo:coefficients]</a> implies $0\le s_1\le1$, $0\le s_2\le s_1^2/3\le1/3$, and $s_3\ge0$. Expanding in these elementary symmetric functions gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{j<k\le3}(1+a_j)^2(1+a_k)^2
 &=3+4s_1+2s_1^2+2s_1s_2+s_2^2-(6+2s_1)s_3\\
 &\le3+4+2+\frac23+\frac19=\frac{88}{9}<10.
\end{aligned}$$ Squaring <a href="#geo:pair-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:pair-bound">[geo:pair-bound]</a>, summing over the three pairs, and combining with <a href="#geo:correlation-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:correlation-lower">[geo:correlation-lower]</a> proves <a href="#geo:second-constraint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:second-constraint">[geo:second-constraint]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="elim:section"></a>
### Excluding five or more active cells

<a id="elim:budget"></a>
We show that the scalar bounds of Lemma <a href="#sc:bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:bounds">4</a> and the geometric constraints of Corollary <a href="#geo:constraints" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:constraints">7</a> are incompatible. We first confine the smallest radius to $.066<t<.12$, and then prove $c>2.6t$, contradicting the second determinant constraint. Throughout this section, the radii are ordered as $$r_1\ge\cdots\ge r_m=t>0,\qquad
 \sum_{j=1}^m r_j^2=1,\qquad
 L=r_1^2+r_2^2,\qquad c=r_3^2.$$ Set <a id="elim:parameters"></a>$$\label{elim:parameters}
 A=\begin{cases}.929,&m=5,\\.884,&m\ge6,\end{cases}
 \qquad d=A-.15,\qquad s=\frac{.415}{d},
 \qquad e(u)=.415u-du^2.$$ The probability excesses $\delta_j=P_j-Ar_j^2$ satisfy, by <a href="#sc:three-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:three-bounds">[sc:three-bounds]</a>, $$\label{elim:budget}
 \delta_j\ge\max\{0,e(r_j)\},\qquad
 \sum_{j=1}^m\delta_j=1-A.$$ All terminating decimals below denote exact rational numbers. The finite comparisons are recorded with strict lower bounds; the rational arithmetic and square-root enclosures that verify them are described in Appendix <a href="#cert:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:arithmetic">7</a>.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### The third radius and the small-minimum case

<a id="elim:common-charge"></a>
<a id="elim:third-radius"></a>
First we obtain a bound that holds for every value of $t$. For $j\ge3$, the inequality $r_j\le\sqrt c$ gives $$e(r_j)=r_j^2\left(\frac{.415}{r_j}-d\right)
 \ge r_j^2\left(\frac{.415}{\sqrt c}-d\right).$$ Summing these charges in <a href="#elim:budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="elim:budget">[elim:budget]</a> yields $$(1-L)\left(\frac{.415}{\sqrt c}-d\right)\le1-A.$$ If $A=.929$, add $.045(1-L)$ to both sides and bound the added right-hand term by $.045$; if $A=.884$, no adjustment is needed. Thus in both cases $$\label{elim:common-charge}
 (1-L)\left(\frac{.415}{\sqrt c}-.734\right)\le.116.$$ Since $r_j\le2/3$, we have $L\le8/9$. If $\sqrt c\le.23$, the left side of <a href="#elim:common-charge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="elim:common-charge">[elim:common-charge]</a> exceeds its right side by at least $$\frac19\left(\frac{.415}{.23}-.734\right)-.116
 =\frac{101}{34500}>0.$$ Consequently $$\label{elim:third-radius}
 \sqrt c>.23.$$

<a id="elim:first-constraint"></a>
Suppose now that $t\le q_0:=.066$. We contradict <a href="#geo:first-constraint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:first-constraint">[geo:first-constraint]</a>, which reads $$\label{elim:first-constraint}
 (c-t^2)(L-c-2t^2)\le8(3/\pi)^2t^2.$$ If $\sqrt c\ge.44$, then $L\ge2c$, so the left side is at least $$(c-t^2)(c-2t^2)
 \ge(.44^2-q_0^2)(.44^2-2q_0^2).$$ Both factors here are positive, and <a id="elim:large-third-small-minimum"></a>$$\label{elim:large-third-small-minimum}
 (.44^2-q_0^2)(.44^2-2q_0^2)
       -8(3/\pi)^2q_0^2>.00321132.$$ This contradicts <a href="#elim:first-constraint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="elim:first-constraint">[elim:first-constraint]</a>.

<a id="elim:minimum-lower"></a>
For the remaining range $.23<\sqrt c<.44$, use the four intervals in Table <a href="#elim:small-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="elim:small-table">2</a>. On an interval $[a,b]$, the denominator $.415/\sqrt c-.734$ is positive, and <a href="#elim:common-charge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="elim:common-charge">[elim:common-charge]</a> gives $$L-c-2t^2
 \ge 1-\frac{.116}{.415/b-.734}-b^2-2q_0^2
 =:M(b).$$ Also $c-t^2\ge a^2-q_0^2>0$. Every $M(b)$ is positive, and the last column of the table contradicts <a href="#elim:first-constraint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="elim:first-constraint">[elim:first-constraint]</a>. Thus $$\label{elim:minimum-lower}
 t>.066.$$

<div id="elim:small-table">

|                                   |          |             |
|:---------------------------------:|:--------:|:-----------:|
|              $[a,b]$              |          |             |
|              $M(b)$               |          |             |
| $(a^2-q_0^2)M(b)-8(3/\pi)^2q_0^2$ |          |             |
|            $[.23,.30]$            | $.72264$ | $.00330237$ |
|            $[.30,.35]$            | $.61198$ | $.02063552$ |
|            $[.35,.41]$            | $.40621$ | $.01621418$ |
|            $[.41,.44]$            | $.24314$ | $.00803616$ |

Strict lower bounds excluding $t\le.066$ when $\sqrt c<.44$.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Bounding the remaining radii

<a id="elim:tail-cap"></a>
<a id="elim:clipped-charge"></a>
<a id="elim:four-charge"></a>
We next prove that $$\label{elim:tail-cap}
 r_j\le.42\qquad(j\ge4).$$ Suppose instead that $r_4>.42$, and put $$K=\frac{d\cdot.42}{s+.42}.$$ For every $u\ge.42$ we have $$\label{elim:clipped-charge}
 \max\{0,e(u)\}\ge K(s^2-u^2).$$ Indeed, when $u\le s$, use $$e(u)=\frac{du}{s+u}(s^2-u^2)
 \quad\hbox{and}\quad \frac{u}{s+u}\ge\frac{.42}{s+.42}.$$ When $u>s$, the right side of <a href="#elim:clipped-charge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="elim:clipped-charge">[elim:clipped-charge]</a> is negative, so nonnegativity proves the inequality. Applying this to the first four radii and using the minimum radius on the others gives $$\sum_{j=1}^4\delta_j
 \ge K\left(4s^2-\sum_{j=1}^4r_j^2\right)
 \ge K\bigl(4s^2-1+(m-4)t^2\bigr).$$ For $j\ge5$, ordering and normalization give $r_j\le1/\sqrt5$. By <a href="#elim:minimum-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="elim:minimum-lower">[elim:minimum-lower]</a>, these radii lie in $[.066,1/\sqrt5]$. Concavity of $e$ therefore gives $$\delta_j\ge\min\{e(.066),e(1/\sqrt5)\}=e(.066).$$ The last equality follows from Table <a href="#elim:four-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="elim:four-table">3</a>, where $e(.066)=.023996676$ for $A=.929$ and $e(.066)=.024192696$ for $A=.884$. Combining the charges and replacing $t$ by $.066$ proves $$\label{elim:four-charge}
 1-A\ge K\bigl(4s^2-1+(m-4)(.066)^2\bigr)+(m-4)e(.066).$$ The right side increases with $m$, with increment $K(.066)^2+e(.066)>0$. At the smallest permitted $m$, its excess over $1-A$ is already positive by Table <a href="#elim:four-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="elim:four-table">3</a>. This contradiction proves <a href="#elim:tail-cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="elim:tail-cap">[elim:tail-cap]</a>.

<div id="elim:four-table">

|                                                                                                                              |              |             |             |
|:----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------:|:------------:|:-----------:|:-----------:|
|                                                             $A$                                                              | smallest $m$ |             |             |
|                                                        $e(1/\sqrt5)$                                                         |              |             |             |
| <a href="#elim:four-charge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="elim:four-charge">[elim:four-charge]</a> minus $1-A$ |              |             |             |
|                                                            $.929$                                                            |     $5$      | $.02979364$ | $.00092980$ |
|                                                            $.884$                                                            |     $6$      | $.03879364$ | $.02229774$ |

Strict lower bounds excluding four radii greater than $.42$.

</div>

<a id="elim:cap-budget"></a>
<a id="elim:tzero"></a>
Put $k=2$ when $m=5$ and $k=3$ when $m\ge6$. There are at least $k$ indices after the first three, and their radii belong to $[t,.42]$. Their charges are each at least $\min\{e(t),e(.42)\}$. Moreover, $$\label{elim:cap-budget}
 ke(.42)-(1-A)=
 \begin{cases}.0027688,&A=.929,\\.0184672,&A=.884.
 \end{cases}$$ Hence <a href="#elim:budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="elim:budget">[elim:budget]</a> forces $ke(t)\le1-A$. Let $t_0$ be the smaller root of $ke(u)=1-A$: $$\label{elim:tzero}
 t_0=\frac{.415-\sqrt{.415^2-4d(1-A)/k}}{2d}.$$ Equation <a href="#elim:cap-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="elim:cap-budget">[elim:cap-budget]</a> places $.42$ strictly between the two roots of this concave quadratic. Since $t\le.42$, the larger-root alternative is excluded, and $t\le t_0$. The enclosures <a id="elim:tzero-enclosures"></a>$$\label{elim:tzero-enclosures}
 \begin{aligned}
 .1070554<t_0<.1070555&\qquad(A=.929),\\
 .1176565<t_0<.1176566&\qquad(A=.884)
 \end{aligned}$$ imply $t_0<.109$ in the first case and $t_0<.12$ in both cases. Furthermore, $e(.42)>(1-A)/k\ge e(t)$, so each of the $k$ selected indices contributes at least $e(t)$.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### The final contradiction

<a id="elim:third-versus-minimum"></a>
<a id="elim:last-budget"></a>
We claim that $$\label{elim:third-versus-minimum}
 c>2.6t.$$ Otherwise, <a href="#elim:third-radius" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="elim:third-radius">[elim:third-radius]</a> places the third radius in $[.23,\sqrt{2.6t}]$. It is distinct from the $k$ indices just charged, so concavity of $e$ and <a href="#elim:budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="elim:budget">[elim:budget]</a> imply $$\label{elim:last-budget}
 1-A\ge\min\{e(.23),e(\sqrt{2.6t})\}+ke(t).$$ We check both possible endpoint charges on the entire interval $[.066,t_0]$.

First, $e$ is increasing up to $t_0$, the smaller root in <a href="#elim:tzero" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="elim:tzero">[elim:tzero]</a>. Thus $$e(.23)+ke(t)-(1-A)
 \ge e(.23)+ke(.066)-(1-A)>0,$$ by Table <a href="#elim:final-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="elim:final-table">4</a>. Second, the function $$F(t)=e(\sqrt{2.6t})+ke(t)
     =.415\sqrt{2.6t}-2.6dt+k(.415t-dt^2)$$ is strictly concave for $t>0$, since $$F''(t)=-\frac{.415\sqrt{2.6}}{4t^{3/2}}-2kd<0.$$ Its minimum on $[.066,t_0]$ is therefore attained at an endpoint. Table <a href="#elim:final-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="elim:final-table">4</a> gives $F(.066)>1-A$ and $2.6t_0<s^2$. The latter implies $e(\sqrt{2.6t_0})>0$; since $ke(t_0)=1-A$, it also gives $F(t_0)>1-A$. These inequalities contradict <a href="#elim:last-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="elim:last-budget">[elim:last-budget]</a> and prove <a href="#elim:third-versus-minimum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="elim:third-versus-minimum">[elim:third-versus-minimum]</a>.

<div id="elim:final-table">

|                         |              |             |             |
|:-----------------------:|:------------:|:-----------:|:-----------:|
|           $A$           |              |             |             |
| $e(.23)+ke(.066)-(1-A)$ |              |             |             |
|     $F(.066)-(1-A)$     |              |             |             |
|      $s^2-2.6t_0$       |              |             |             |
|         $.929$          | $.031234252$ | $.01522916$ | $.00546157$ |
|         $.884$          | $.013199488$ | $.00253590$ | $.01376450$ |

Positive endpoint margins for <a href="#elim:last-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="elim:last-budget">[elim:last-budget]</a>. The second column is exact; the last two columns give strict lower bounds.

</div>

Finally, <a href="#geo:second-constraint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:second-constraint">[geo:second-constraint]</a>, <a href="#elim:third-versus-minimum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="elim:third-versus-minimum">[elim:third-versus-minimum]</a>, and $0<t<.12$ give $$10(3/\pi)^2t^2
 \ge1.5(c-t^2)^2
 >1.5(2.6-.12)^2t^2.$$ But $$1.5(2.6-.12)^2-10(3/\pi)^2>.10667806>0.$$ This contradiction rules out $C>B^2$ and completes the upper bound.

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-constraints-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-constraints</summary>

*Completion of the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Section <a href="#elim:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="elim:section">5</a> rules out $C>9/(8\pi)$ for the supremum defined in Section <a href="#ext:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:section">2</a>. The product-and-empty-cell reduction at the start of that section transfers this bound to every dimension and finite cell count. The construction in Section <a href="#sec:example" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:example">1.1</a> attains the bound whenever $d\ge2$ and $k\ge3$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Gaussian maxima

The partition bound also gives a sharp inequality for Gaussian maxima. The four-score version appears in Heilman, Jagannath, and Naor <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[HJN](#ref-HJN), Theorem 1.2]; Gaussian partition duality is due to Khot and Naor <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[KhotNaor](#ref-KhotNaor), Lemma 3.7]. Subtracting the common average score gives the following centered form in every dimension.

<a id="stmt-cor-gaussian-maxima"></a>
<div id="cor:gaussian-maxima" class="corollary">

**Corollary 8** (Gaussian maxima). *Let $d,k$ be positive integers, let $g$ be standard Gaussian in $\mathbb R^d$, and let $v_1,\ldots,v_k\in\mathbb R^d$, with $\bar v=k^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^k v_i$. Then $$\mathbb E\max_{1\le i\le k}\langle v_i,g\rangle
 \le\frac{3}{2\sqrt{2\pi}}
       \left(\sum_{i=1}^k\|v_i-\bar v\|^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ The constant is sharp already for three vectors in the plane.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-gaussian-maxima"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-gaussian-maxima</summary>

*Proof.* Put $u_i=v_i-\bar v$. Subtracting the common score $\langle\bar v,g\rangle$ leaves the expected maximum unchanged. Partition $\mathbb R^d$ according to the largest score $\langle u_i,x\rangle$, resolving ties by the smallest index, and let $z_i$ be the Gaussian first moments of these cells. Then Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> and Cauchy–Schwarz give $$\mathbb E\max_i\langle v_i,g\rangle
 =\sum_i\langle u_i,z_i\rangle
 \le\left(\sum_i\|u_i\|^2\right)^{1/2}
      \left(\sum_i\|z_i\|^2\right)^{1/2}
 \le\frac{3}{2\sqrt{2\pi}}
      \left(\sum_i\|u_i\|^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ For sharpness, take the three centroid vectors of the planar propeller in Section <a href="#sec:example" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:example">1.1</a>. Their sum is zero, their score cells are the propeller sectors, and both inequalities are equalities. ◻

</details>

Every finite centered jointly Gaussian vector $(X_1,\ldots,X_k)$ can be written as $(\langle v_i,g\rangle)_{i=1}^k$, including when its covariance is singular. Thus the corollary also bounds its expected maximum by $3/(2\sqrt{2\pi})$ times $\bigl(\sum_i\operatorname{Var}(X_i-\bar X)\bigr)^{1/2}$, where $\bar X=k^{-1}\sum_iX_i$.

<a id="kc:section"></a>
### A kernel-clustering application

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### The objective and the approximation threshold

This application uses Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, the kernel-clustering results of Khot and Naor, and the companion paper *The Unique Games Theorem*. None of these complexity arguments is used in the proof of the Gaussian inequality.

<a id="kc:value"></a>
Fix an integer $k\ge3$. An input is an explicitly represented rational symmetric matrix $A=(a_{pq})_{p,q=1}^N$ satisfying $A\succeq0$ and $A\mathbf 1=0$. The latter is the centering condition; for a positive semidefinite matrix it is equivalent to $\sum_{p,q}a_{pq}=0$. For a map $\sigma:[N]\to[k]$, define $$\label{kc:value}
 \operatorname{val}_A(\sigma)=\sum_{p,q=1}^N a_{pq}\mathbf 1_{\{\sigma(p)=\sigma(q)\}},
 \qquad
 \operatorname{Clust}(A\mid I_k)=\max_{\sigma:[N]\to[k]}\operatorname{val}_A(\sigma).$$ Here $[m]=\{1,\ldots,m\}$, and $I_k$ is the $k$-by-$k$ identity target matrix. The sum is over ordered pairs and includes the diagonal. Equivalently, it is $\sum_{i=1}^k\sum_{p,q\in S_i}a_{pq}$ for the cells $S_i=\sigma^{-1}(i)$; empty cells are allowed. There is no normalization by cell cardinalities. Each objective value is nonnegative, since it is a sum of quadratic forms $\mathbf 1_{S_i}^{\mathsf T}A\mathbf 1_{S_i}$.

A deterministic approximation with *loss factor* $\alpha\ge1$ returns an assignment of value at least $\operatorname{Clust}(A\mid I_k)/\alpha$. An expected approximation has the same inequality with the returned value replaced by its expectation. Put $$c_*=\frac9{8\pi},
 \qquad
 \alpha_k=\frac{1-1/k}{c_*}
          =\frac{8\pi}{9}\left(1-\frac1k\right).$$

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
###### The matching rounding constant.

Khot and Naor’s approximation theorem <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[KhotNaor](#ref-KhotNaor), Theorem 2.1] gives the expected rounding bound $$\mathbb E\operatorname{val}_A(\sigma)\ge\frac{\operatorname{Clust}(A\mid I_k)}{\alpha_k}$$ on the inputs in <a href="#kc:value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:value">[kc:value]</a>. This is their endpoint analytic guarantee using an optimal semidefinite relaxation and ideal Gaussian sampling. We quote this rounding guarantee separately from the rational-input hardness statement below; an exact finite-bit implementation at the endpoint is not asserted here.

<a id="stmt-kc-main"></a>
<div id="kc:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 9** (Kernel-clustering hardness). *For each fixed $k\ge3$ and every fixed $1\le\alpha<\alpha_k$, it is NP-hard, given a rational symmetric matrix $A\succeq0$ with $A\mathbf 1=0$ and the identity-target objective <a href="#kc:value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:value">[kc:value]</a>, to find $\sigma:[N]\to[k]$ such that $$\operatorname{val}_A(\sigma)\ge\operatorname{Clust}(A\mid I_k)/\alpha.$$ In particular, such a deterministic polynomial-time approximation would imply $\mathrm P=\mathrm{NP}$.*

</div>

We prove the hardness statement below. It concerns strict improvement over $\alpha_k$, not hardness at the endpoint.

For the more general objective $\sum_{p,q}a_{pq}b_{\sigma(p)\sigma(q)}$ with a fixed positive semidefinite target matrix $B=(b_{ij})_{i,j=1}^k$, Khot and Naor later characterized the Unique Games hardness threshold for approximating the optimal clustering value through geometric and Gaussian partition parameters <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[KhotNaorSharp](#ref-KhotNaorSharp), Theorem 1.1]. Here the target is $I_k$, and the Gaussian constant is supplied by Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### The two external hardness inputs

<a id="kc:gaussian-constant"></a>
The Gaussian parameter in Khot and Naor’s theorem is $$C_k:=\sup_{(B_1,\ldots,B_k)}
 \sum_{i=1}^k
 \left\|\int_{B_i}x\,d\gamma_{k-1}(x)\right\|^2.$$ The supremum ranges over measurable partitions of $\mathbb R^{k-1}$, with empty cells allowed. Since $k\ge3$, Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> and the attaining example in Section <a href="#sec:example" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:example">1.1</a> give $$\label{kc:gaussian-constant}
 C_k=c_*.$$

<a id="kc:influence"></a>
We first specify the discrete analytic input. Let $\Omega=[k]$ have uniform probability measure. Choose a real orthonormal basis $b_0,b_1,\ldots,b_{k-1}$ of functions on $\Omega$, with $b_0=1$. For $r\in\mathbb N$ and $\nu\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}^r$, write $$b_\nu(x)=\prod_{j=1}^r b_{\nu_j}(x_j),
 \qquad
 |\nu|=\bigl|\{j:\nu_j\ne0\}\bigr|.$$ Every $h:\Omega^r\to\mathbb R$ has expansion $h=\sum_\nu\widehat h(\nu)b_\nu$, with coefficients computed using uniform probability measure. For real $D\ge0$, set $$\label{kc:influence}
 \operatorname{Inf}_j^{\le D}(h)=
 \sum_{\substack{|\nu|\le D\\\nu_j\ne0}}\widehat h(\nu)^2.$$ Thus a nonintegral cutoff is interpreted by the displayed inequality on the integer $|\nu|$. Let $P_1$ be orthogonal projection onto the span of $b_\nu$ with $|\nu|=1$, and, for $f=(f_1,\ldots,f_k)$, put $$\operatorname{OBJ}(f)=\sum_{i=1}^k\|P_1f_i\|_2^2.$$ All $L_2$ norms in this section use uniform probability measure. Define the closed simplex with slack by $$\Delta_k=\{u\in\mathbb R^k:u_i\ge0,\ \sum_{i=1}^k u_i\le1\}.$$

<a id="stmt-kc-kn-input"></a>
<div id="kc:kn-input" class="theorem">

**Theorem 10** (Khot–Naor low-influence bound). *For fixed $k$ and every $\eta>0$, there exists $0<\tau_0<1/2$, independent of $r$, such that every $r\in\mathbb N$ and every $f:\Omega^r\to\Delta_k$ satisfying $$\operatorname{Inf}_j^{\le\log(1/\tau_0)}(f_i)\le\tau_0
 \quad(i\in[k],\ j\in[r])$$ obey $\operatorname{OBJ}(f)\le C_k+\eta$.*

</div>

<a id="kc:cutoff"></a>
<a id="kc:contrapositive"></a>
This is <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[KhotNaor](#ref-KhotNaor), Theorem 3.8], with the same uniform product measure, Fourier degree, simplex, and Gaussian normalization. Its proof uses the invariance principle of Mossel, O’Donnell, and Oleszkiewicz <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[MOO](#ref-MOO)] to compare low-influence functions on product spaces with their Gaussian counterparts. The restriction $\tau_0<1/2$ is harmless: decreasing the threshold and increasing the degree cutoff strengthen the hypotheses. Choose a rational $0<\tau<\min\{\tau_0,1/2\}$ and set $$\label{kc:cutoff}
 D=\max\{1,\lceil\log(1/\tau)\rceil\}.$$ Monotonicity of <a href="#kc:influence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:influence">[kc:influence]</a> in the cutoff gives the useful contrapositive $$\label{kc:contrapositive}
 \operatorname{OBJ}(f)>C_k+\eta
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \operatorname{Inf}_j^{\le D}(f_i)>\tau
 \text{ for some }i\in[k],\ j\in[r].$$ Indeed, if every influence at cutoff $D$ were at most $\tau$, every influence at the original cutoff would be at most $\tau_0$.

The second input is the following exact interface of <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[UniqueGamesTheorem](#ref-UniqueGamesTheorem), Theorem 1.1].

<a id="stmt-kc-ug-input"></a>
<div id="kc:ug-input" class="theorem">

**Theorem 11** (Unique Games input). *For every fixed $\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1/2)$, there are an integer $s\ge1$ and a deterministic polynomial-time reduction from $\mathrm{3SAT}$ to explicit unweighted simple bipartite Unique Games instances over $\mathbb F_2^s$. If the formula is satisfiable the game has a labeling satisfying at least $1-\varepsilon$ of its edges; if it is unsatisfiable every labeling satisfies at most $\delta$ of its edges. The edge set is nonempty and each constraint is a translation of the alphabet. The alphabet size and the polynomial’s degree and constants depend only on $\varepsilon,\delta$.*

</div>

Only ordinary satisfied-edge fractions are asserted here. We will use neither regularity nor the stronger condition that almost every left vertex has all its incident constraints satisfied.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### A rational centered positive semidefinite matrix

Let $G=(V,W,E)$ be a game supplied by Theorem <a href="#kc:ug-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kc:ug-input">11</a>, and enumerate its alphabet as $[r]$, where $r=2^s$. Reorient constraints if necessary so that the permutation $\pi_{vw}:[r]\to[r]$ attached to $vw\in E$ is satisfied precisely when $$\ell(v)=\pi_{vw}(\ell(w)).$$ Translations are permutations, so this changes no game value. Delete isolated vertices, put $M=|E|$, and write $$d_v=|N(v)|,\qquad \mu(v)=\frac{d_v}{M}\quad(v\in V).$$ Choosing $v$ with probability $\mu(v)$, then a uniform $w\in N(v)$, chooses an edge uniformly.

<a id="kc:averaging"></a>
To fix the permutation convention, for a permutation $\pi$ define $$(R_\pi x)_a=x_{\pi(a)}\quad(a\in[r]).$$ For a scalar table $h:W\times\Omega^r\to\mathbb R$, define $$\label{kc:averaging}
 (T_vh)(x)=\frac1{d_v}
       \sum_{w\in N(v)}h(w,R_{\pi_{vw}}x),
 \qquad
 Q(h)=\sum_{v\in V}\mu(v)\|P_1T_vh\|_2^2 .$$ The output matrix will represent $Q$ in the ordinary, unnormalized Euclidean coordinates of the table $h$.

<a id="stmt-kc-matrix"></a>
<div id="kc:matrix" class="lemma">

<a id="kc:objective-identity"></a>
**Lemma 12**. *For fixed $k,r$, one can construct in deterministic polynomial time a rational symmetric matrix $A$, indexed by $W\times\Omega^r$, such that $$h^{\mathsf T}Ah=Q(h),\qquad A\succeq0,\qquad A\mathbf 1=0 .$$ For every $\sigma:W\times\Omega^r\to[k]$, if $F_i(w,x)=\mathbf 1_{\{\sigma(w,x)=i\}}$, then $$\label{kc:objective-identity}
 \operatorname{val}_A(\sigma)=
 \sum_{v\in V}\mu(v)\operatorname{OBJ}(F_v),\qquad
 F_v=(T_vF_1,\ldots,T_vF_k):\Omega^r\to\Delta_k.$$ All output assignment values lie in $[0,1]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-kc-matrix"><summary>Proof of stmt-kc-matrix</summary>

<a id="kc:projection-matrix"></a>
<a id="kc:matrix-formula"></a>
*Proof.* Put $q=k^r$. The projection $P_1$, written as a matrix acting on the counting-coordinate vector $(h(x))_{x\in\Omega^r}$, is $$\label{kc:projection-matrix}
 (P_1)_{x,y}=\frac1q
       \sum_{j=1}^r\bigl(k\mathbf 1_{\{x_j=y_j\}}-1\bigr).$$ To see this, the centered subspaces of functions of distinct individual coordinates are mutually orthogonal. Hence $$(P_1h)(x)=
 \sum_{j=1}^r\bigl(\mathbb E[h(X)\mid X_j=x_j]-\mathbb Eh(X)\bigr),$$ which is <a href="#kc:projection-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:projection-matrix">[kc:projection-matrix]</a>. This matrix is rational, symmetric and idempotent. The counting-coordinate matrix of $T_v$ has entries $$(T_v)_{x,(w,y)}
 =\frac1{d_v}\mathbf 1_{\{w\in N(v)\}}
           \mathbf 1_{\{y=R_{\pi_{vw}}x\}} .$$ Define $$\label{kc:matrix-formula}
 A=\frac1q\sum_{v\in V}\mu(v)T_v^{\mathsf T}P_1T_v.$$ There are two factors $q^{-1}$: one in <a href="#kc:projection-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:projection-matrix">[kc:projection-matrix]</a>, and one outside the sum in <a href="#kc:matrix-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:matrix-formula">[kc:matrix-formula]</a> because $L_2$ uses probability measure. Symmetry and $P_1^2=P_1$ give $$h^{\mathsf T}Ah
 =\sum_v\mu(v)\frac1q\|P_1T_vh\|_{\ell_2}^2=Q(h)\ge0.$$ Also $T_v\mathbf 1=\mathbf 1$ and $P_1\mathbf 1=0$, so <a href="#kc:matrix-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:matrix-formula">[kc:matrix-formula]</a> gives $A\mathbf 1=0$.

There are $N=|W|k^r$ output indices. For fixed $k,r$ this is polynomial in the explicit game size, and the dense matrix can be computed by rational arithmetic in polynomial time. Its entries are sums of terms with denominators dividing $M d_v q^2$. A common denominator divides $M q^2\prod_{v\in V}d_v$, whose bit length is $O(\log M+\sum_v\log d_v+\log q)$, and the numerators also have polynomial bit length. No irrational Fourier basis needs to be constructed.

<a id="kc:objective-bound"></a>
By definition of the ordered sum, $\operatorname{val}_A(\sigma)=\sum_iF_i^{\mathsf T}AF_i$; inserting <a href="#kc:averaging" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:averaging">[kc:averaging]</a> proves <a href="#kc:objective-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:objective-identity">[kc:objective-identity]</a>. Every $F_v(x)$ is an average of coordinate unit vectors, so its entries are nonnegative and sum to one. Parseval and orthogonal projection give $$\label{kc:objective-bound}
 0\le\operatorname{OBJ}(F_v)
 \le\sum_{i=1}^k\|F_{v,i}\|_2^2
 \le\mathbb E\sum_{i=1}^k F_{v,i}=1.$$ Since $\sum_v\mu(v)=1$, the last claim follows. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Completeness and weighted decoding

The next two lemmas compare ordinary edge-value completeness and soundness with the kernel objective. They adapt the Khot–Naor reduction <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[KhotNaor](#ref-KhotNaor), Section 3.3] to the precise input of Theorem <a href="#kc:ug-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kc:ug-input">11</a>.

<a id="stmt-kc-completeness"></a>
<div id="kc:completeness" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13** (Completeness). *If a labeling of $G$ satisfies at least $1-\varepsilon$ of its edges, then the matrix of Lemma <a href="#kc:matrix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kc:matrix">12</a> satisfies $$\operatorname{Clust}(A\mid I_k)\ge
       \left(1-\frac1k\right)(1-\varepsilon)^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-kc-completeness"><summary>Proof of stmt-kc-completeness</summary>

*Proof.* Use a witnessing labeling $\ell$ to set $\sigma(w,x)=x_{\ell(w)}$. For $j\in[r]$, let $D_j(x)=e_{x_j}$, where $e_1,\ldots,e_k$ are the coordinate unit vectors in $\mathbb R^k$. With $$p_{v,j}=
 \Pr_{w\in N(v)}\{\pi_{vw}(\ell(w))=j\},$$ the chosen permutation convention gives $F_v=\sum_{j=1}^r p_{v,j}D_j$. For each $i$, the level-one part of the $i$-th component of $D_j$ is $\mathbf 1_{\{x_j=i\}}-1/k$. These parts for different $j$’s are orthogonal. Moreover, $$\sum_{i=1}^k
 \mathbb E\bigl(\mathbf 1_{\{X_j=i\}}-1/k\bigr)^2=1-\frac1k.$$ It follows that $$\operatorname{OBJ}(F_v)=
 \left(1-\frac1k\right)\sum_{j=1}^r p_{v,j}^2
 \ge \left(1-\frac1k\right)p_{v,\ell(v)}^2.$$ The weighted mean $\mathbb E_{v\sim\mu}p_{v,\ell(v)}$ is exactly the satisfied-edge fraction of $\ell$, and is therefore at least $1-\varepsilon$. Jensen’s inequality and <a href="#kc:objective-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:objective-identity">[kc:objective-identity]</a> prove the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-kc-soundness"></a>
<div id="kc:soundness" class="lemma">

<a id="kc:delta"></a>
**Lemma 14** (Soundness). *Fix $\eta>0$ with $C_k+2\eta<1$, and choose $\tau,D$ as in <a href="#kc:cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:cutoff">[kc:cutoff]</a> for this $\eta$. If every labeling of $G$ satisfies at most $\delta$ of its edges, where $$\label{kc:delta}
 0<\delta<\frac{\eta\tau^2}{4kD},$$ then $\operatorname{Clust}(A\mid I_k)\le C_k+2\eta$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-kc-soundness"><summary>Proof of stmt-kc-soundness</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose an assignment $\sigma$ gives a larger value, and use the functions in Lemma <a href="#kc:matrix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kc:matrix">12</a>. Let $$V_{\mathrm{good}}=
       \{v:\operatorname{OBJ}(F_v)>C_k+\eta\}.$$ If its $\mu$-mass is $\beta$, then <a href="#kc:objective-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:objective-bound">[kc:objective-bound]</a> gives $$\sum_v\mu(v)\operatorname{OBJ}(F_v)
 \le\beta+(1-\beta)(C_k+\eta)
 \le C_k+\eta+\beta.$$ The assumed objective therefore forces $\beta>\eta$.

<a id="kc:influence-average"></a>
For every $v\in V_{\mathrm{good}}$, <a href="#kc:contrapositive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:contrapositive">[kc:contrapositive]</a> supplies $i_v\in[k]$ and $j_v\in[r]$ with $\operatorname{Inf}_{j_v}^{\le D}(F_{v,i_v})>\tau$. Taking the squared norm of the relevant Fourier coefficients in the neighbor average, and using convexity, gives $$\label{kc:influence-average}
 \operatorname{Inf}_{j_v}^{\le D}(F_{v,i_v})
 \le \frac1{d_v}\sum_{w\in N(v)}
       \operatorname{Inf}_{\pi_{vw}^{-1}(j_v)}^{\le D}(F_{i_v}(w,\cdot)).$$ The inverse permutation in this formula follows directly from $(R_\pi x)_a=x_{\pi(a)}$: the Fourier indices involving left coordinate $j_v$ come from right coordinate $\pi^{-1}(j_v)$. Every influence on the right belongs to $[0,1]$, by Parseval and $0\le F_i(w,\cdot)\le1$. An average exceeding $\tau$ has more than a $\tau/2$ fraction of its entries at least $\tau/2$: otherwise its average is at most $\tau/2+(\tau/2)\cdot1\le\tau$.

<a id="kc:list-size"></a>
For each $w\in W$, form the finite label list $$L(w)=
 \{a\in[r]:\operatorname{Inf}_a^{\le D}(F_i(w,\cdot))\ge\tau/2
                      \text{ for some }i\in[k]\}.$$ For every scalar $h$, counting how often a Fourier coefficient contributes gives $$\sum_{a=1}^r\operatorname{Inf}_a^{\le D}(h)
 =\sum_{|\nu|\le D}|\nu|\,\widehat h(\nu)^2
 \le D\|h\|_2^2.$$ Consequently $$\label{kc:list-size}
 |L(w)|\frac{\tau}{2}
 \le\sum_{i=1}^k\sum_{a=1}^r
            \operatorname{Inf}_a^{\le D}(F_i(w,\cdot))
 \le kD,
 \qquad |L(w)|\le\frac{2kD}{\tau}.$$

Label a good $v$ by $j_v$, and other left vertices arbitrarily. Independently label each right vertex uniformly from $L(w)$ when this list is nonempty, and arbitrarily otherwise. For a good $v$, more than a $\tau/2$ fraction of its neighbors have $\pi_{vw}^{-1}(j_v)\in L(w)$, by <a href="#kc:influence-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:influence-average">[kc:influence-average]</a>. On each such edge the random label at $w$ satisfies the constraint with probability at least $\tau/(2kD)$, by <a href="#kc:list-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:list-size">[kc:list-size]</a>. The expected fraction of satisfied edges is therefore greater than $$\eta\cdot\frac{\tau}{2}\cdot\frac{\tau}{2kD}
   =\frac{\eta\tau^2}{4kD}.$$ Here the weights $\mu(v)=d_v/M$ ensure that this is the uniform-edge fraction. Some deterministic labeling attains at least its expectation, contradicting <a href="#kc:delta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:delta">[kc:delta]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Completing the reduction

<details id="proof-stmt-kc-soundness-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-kc-soundness</summary>

<a id="kc:separated-gap"></a>
*Proof of the hardness assertion in Theorem <a href="#kc:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kc:main">9</a>.* Fix $k\ge3$ and $1\le\alpha<\alpha_k$. First choose rational $\varepsilon,\eta>0$ sufficiently small that $\varepsilon<1/2$, $c_*+2\eta<1$, and $$\label{kc:separated-gap}
 \alpha<
 \frac{(1-1/k)(1-\varepsilon)^2}{c_*+2\eta}.$$ Next choose $\tau,D$ from Theorem <a href="#kc:kn-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kc:kn-input">10</a> and <a href="#kc:cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:cutoff">[kc:cutoff]</a>; these depend on $k,\eta$, not the game size. Choose a rational $$0<\delta<
 \min\left\{\frac12,\frac{\eta\tau^2}{4kD}\right\}.$$ Apply Theorem <a href="#kc:ug-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kc:ug-input">11</a> with these fixed errors. Only at this stage is the fixed alphabet size $r=2^s$ determined. In particular $k^r$ is a constant with respect to the input formula size, although no uniform bound polynomial in the error reciprocals is asserted.

Compose that deterministic reduction with Lemma <a href="#kc:matrix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kc:matrix">12</a>. Lemmas <a href="#kc:completeness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kc:completeness">13</a> and <a href="#kc:soundness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kc:soundness">14</a>, together with <a href="#kc:gaussian-constant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:gaussian-constant">[kc:gaussian-constant]</a>, give the gap $$\begin{aligned}
 \varphi\text{ satisfiable}
 &\ \Longrightarrow\
 \operatorname{Clust}(A_\varphi\mid I_k)\ge
       H:=(1-1/k)(1-\varepsilon)^2,\\
 \varphi\text{ unsatisfiable}
 &\ \Longrightarrow\
 \operatorname{Clust}(A_\varphi\mid I_k)\le
       S:=c_*+2\eta .
 \end{aligned}$$ The output is a rational symmetric centered PSD matrix with polynomial dimension and bit length. By <a href="#kc:separated-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:separated-gap">[kc:separated-gap]</a>, $S<H/\alpha$. Choose a fixed rational $\theta$ strictly between these numbers. A deterministic loss-factor-$\alpha$ algorithm produces value greater than $\theta$ on satisfiable formulas; every assignment has value less than $\theta$ on unsatisfiable formulas. The returned value is an exactly computable rational number. This would decide $\mathrm{3SAT}$ in polynomial time. Thus achieving the stated factor is NP-hard. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-kc-randomized"></a>
<div id="kc:randomized" class="remark">

*Remark 15* (Randomized guarantees). The quoted Khot–Naor rounding guarantee is in expectation. The deterministic hardness conclusion of Theorem <a href="#kc:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kc:main">9</a> does not use $\mathrm P\ne\mathrm{NP}$ to exclude randomized algorithms. More precisely, a polynomial-time randomized algorithm whose expected value is at least $\operatorname{Clust}(A\mid I_k)/\alpha$ for some fixed $\alpha<\alpha_k$ would place $\mathrm{3SAT}$ in $\mathrm{RP}$. Indeed, on the reduction instances its returned value $X$ lies in $[0,1]$. In the satisfiable case $\mathbb EX\ge H/\alpha>\theta$; therefore $$\Pr\{X>\theta\}\ge
 \frac{H/\alpha-\theta}{1-\theta}>0.$$ This is a fixed positive constant. In the unsatisfiable case $X\le S<\theta$ always. A fixed number of repetitions gives one-sided success probability at least $1/2$.

</div>

<a id="stmt-kc-dependencies"></a>
<div id="kc:dependencies" class="remark">

*Remark 16* (Inputs to the application). The Gaussian proof supplies only <a href="#kc:gaussian-constant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kc:gaussian-constant">[kc:gaussian-constant]</a>. Khot and Naor supply the expected rounding guarantee <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[KhotNaor](#ref-KhotNaor), Theorem 2.1] and the analytic low-influence bound <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[KhotNaor](#ref-KhotNaor), Theorem 3.8]. Their reduction in Section 3.3 is the model for Lemmas <a href="#kc:matrix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kc:matrix">12</a>–<a href="#kc:soundness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kc:soundness">14</a>; the degree weights and the squared completeness loss explicitly accommodate the ordinary edge-value, possibly nonregular instances from <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[UniqueGamesTheorem](#ref-UniqueGamesTheorem), Theorem 1.1]. No stronger Unique Games promise is assumed. The separate Unique Games theorem is a formal dependency only of this hardness application. The algorithmic endpoint is quoted in its ideal rounding formulation; this section supplies no finite-precision endpoint implementation.

</div>

<a id="cert:arithmetic"></a>
### Elementary certificates for the numerical inequalities

This appendix gives rational enclosures for the numerical comparisons used in the proof. All finite decimals denote exact rationals. The formulas below and the tables suffice to reproduce the comparisons; no numerical optimization or numerical quadrature is needed.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Enclosure formulas

<a id="cert:pi"></a>
We use $$\label{cert:pi}
 3.14159<\pi<3.14160.$$ For example, these bounds follow from $\pi=16\arctan(1/5)-4\arctan(1/239)$ by bounding each arctangent between its alternating-series partial sums through indices 7 and 6. All square-root enclosures may be obtained using rational numbers $l,u\ge0$ with $l^2\le x\le u^2$. An explicit rule, if desired, is to set $D=10^{35}$ and use $$\frac{\lfloor\sqrt{\lfloor xD^2\rfloor}\rfloor}{D}
 \le\sqrt{x}\le
 \frac{\lfloor\sqrt{\lfloor xD^2\rfloor}\rfloor+1}{D}.$$ For an interval of possible $x$, apply the lower rule to its lower endpoint and the upper rule to its upper endpoint. Thus only rational operations and integer square roots are required.

<a id="cert:exponential"></a>
<a id="cert:normal"></a>
For $y\ge0$, let $$E_N(y)=\sum_{j=0}^N\frac{(-y)^j}{j!}.$$ Taylor’s integral remainder gives $$\label{cert:exponential}
 E_{15}(y)\le e^{-y}\le E_{14}(y).$$ This conclusion does not require the initial terms of the series to be decreasing. Integrating the same bounds, set $$J_N(x)=\sum_{j=0}^N
 \frac{(-1)^j x^{2j+1}}{2^j j!(2j+1)},\qquad x\ge0.$$ Then $$\label{cert:normal}
 \frac12+\frac{J_{15}(x)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}
 \le\Phi(x)\le
 \frac12+\frac{J_{14}(x)}{\sqrt{2\pi}},\qquad
 \Phi(-x)=1-\Phi(x).$$ Together with <a href="#cert:pi" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:pi">[cert:pi]</a> and the square-root rule, these are rational enclosures for both $\phi$ and $\Phi$ at every argument used below. Interval addition, subtraction, multiplication, and division by intervals avoiding zero preserve the enclosures.

<a id="cert:arcsine"></a>
For the cap calculation, put $$Q(x)=\sum_{j=0}^4\frac{\binom{2j}{j}}{4^j(2j+1)}x^{2j+1}.$$ The binomial series has positive coefficients at most one. Its tail starting in degree 10 is bounded by $x^{10}/(1-x^2)$; after integration this gives, for $0\le x<1$, $$\label{cert:arcsine}
 Q(x)\le\arcsin x\le Q(x)+\frac{x^{11}}{11(1-x^2)}.$$

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### The two rearrangement minima

Write $f(x)=(9/4)e^{x^2}\Phi(-x)$ and $D_f(x)=2x-\phi(x)/\Phi(-x)$, as in <a href="#sc:halfspace-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:halfspace-ratio">[sc:halfspace-ratio]</a>. Substitution into <a href="#cert:exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:exponential">[cert:exponential]</a>–<a href="#cert:normal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:normal">[cert:normal]</a> gives the following strict enclosures: $$\begin{array}{rcl}
 -0.000131&<&D_f(0.6119)<-0.000126,\\
  0.000120&<&D_f(0.6121)< 0.000124,\\
 -0.000430&<&D_f(x)<0.000424
               \quad(0.6119\le x\le0.6121),\\
  0.884383832&<&f(0.612).
\end{array}$$ For the third row, use the whole rational interval $[0.6119,0.6121]$ in the elementary interval operations; $\phi(x)$ decreases and $\Phi(-x)$ decreases there, so endpoint enclosures give both factors. Thus $|D_f|<0.001$ throughout that interval. The minimizer lies within $0.0001$ of $0.612$, and so $$\min f\ge f(0.612)e^{-10^{-7}}
 >0.884383832(1-10^{-7})>0.8843837.$$

For $p,F,H,R$ from <a href="#sc:cap-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:cap-ratio">[sc:cap-ratio]</a> and its following calculation, <a href="#cert:arcsine" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:arcsine">[cert:arcsine]</a> gives $$\begin{array}{rcl}
 -0.0047010&<&F(0.29)<-0.0046981,\\
  0.0007683&<&F(0.294)<0.0007712,\\
  0.9312526&<&R(0.29),\\
  0.22198&<&p(0.294)-0.294/\pi.
\end{array}$$ For $0.29\le x\le0.294$, the last row implies $$H(x)=p(x)-\frac{x\sqrt{1-x^2}}\pi
 \ge p(0.294)-\frac{0.294}\pi>0.$$ Therefore $F$ is increasing throughout the interval, and the first two rows imply $|F(x)|<0.004702$ there. Consequently $$|R'(x)|<\frac{(9/4)0.004702}{(1-0.294^2)^4}
 <0.015189<0.03.$$ This proves the derivative bound on the entire interval. The minimum therefore exceeds $0.9312526-0.03(0.004)=0.9311326$.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Quantiles and the interval probability bounds

For $P_0(a)=(0.415+0.15a)a$, Table <a href="#cert:quantiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:quantiles">5</a> gives a rational $q_a$ satisfying $\Phi(q_a)<P_0(a)$, and a strict upper bound on $G(q_a)$. Each entry follows by substituting the displayed rational argument into <a href="#cert:exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:exponential">[cert:exponential]</a>–<a href="#cert:normal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:normal">[cert:normal]</a> and the definition of $G$. Since $G$ is nonincreasing, $$G\bigl(\Phi^{-1}(P_0(r))\bigr)\le G(q_a)
 \qquad(r\ge a).$$ In each row the listed upper bound is strictly below the value of $U$ in Table <a href="#sc:interval-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:interval-table">1</a>.

<div id="cert:quantiles">

|  $a$ |  $q_a$ | $10^6(P_0(a)-\Phi(q_a))>$ | $G(q_a)<$ |
|-----:|-------:|--------------------------:|----------:|
| 0.05 | -2.033 |                        98 |  0.887728 |
| 0.14 | -1.548 |                       228 |  0.854397 |
| 0.24 | -1.238 |                       381 |  0.826606 |
| 0.33 | -1.024 |                       366 |  0.803691 |
| 0.41 | -0.860 |                       470 |  0.783737 |
| 0.48 | -0.728 |                       452 |  0.766006 |
| 0.54 | -0.621 |                       539 |  0.750456 |
| 0.59 | -0.535 |                       739 |  0.737150 |
| 0.64 | -0.450 |                       684 |  0.723262 |

Rational enclosures certifying the quantile and $G$ bounds.

</div>

Finally, for each row $(a,b,U)$ of Table <a href="#sc:interval-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:interval-table">1</a>, replace $\pi$ by its upper bound in $B^2=9/(8\pi)$, enclose $\sqrt{1-a^2}$ from above, and subtract the exact rational $U(0.415+0.15b)^2$. This gives the lower bounds in that table’s last column. The same rational and square-root rules apply to the finite comparisons in the numerical elimination section.

      "caption": "The attaining planar partition consists of three unbounded sectors $S_{1}, S_{2}, S_{3}$ of angle $2π/3$; only a finite window is shown. The bold arrows show the unit centroid directions $ẑ_{i} = z(S_{i})/∥z(S_{i})∥$. Each unnormalized Gaussian centroid has length $\\sqrt{3}/(2\\sqrt{2\\pi})$, so their squared lengths sum to $9/(8π)$. In dimension $d ≥ 2$, use $S_{i} × ℝ^{d − 2}$, with any further cells empty.",
      "figure_id": "fig:propeller-sectors",
      "pdf_page": 2,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:propeller-sectors",
          "exact_quote": "The attaining planar partition consists of three unbounded\n  sectors $S_1,S_2,S_3$ of angle $2\\pi/3$; only a finite window is shown.\n  The bold arrows show the unit centroid directions\n  $\\widehat z_i=z(S_i)/\\|z(S_i)\\|$.\n  Each unnormalized",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,line cap=round,line join=round,\n                      font=\\small]\n    \\begin{scope}\n      \\clip (-2.35,-1.95) rectangle (2.35,1.95);\n      \\fill[blue!9] (0,0) -- (-60:8) -- (60:8) -- cycle;\n      \\fill[orange!13] (0,0) -- (60:8) -- (180:8) -- cycle;\n      \\fill[green!10!white] (0,0) -- (180:8) -- (300:8) -- cycle;\n    \\end{scope}\n    \\draw[->,thin] (0,0) -- (60:2.17);\n    \\draw[->,thin] (0,0) -- (180:2.30);\n    \\draw[->,thin] (0,0) -- (300:2.17);\n    \\draw[thick,->] (0,0) -- (0:1.20)\n      node[above=3pt] {$\\widehat z_1$};\n    \\draw[thick,->] (0,0) -- (120:1.20)\n      node[left=3pt] {$\\widehat z_2$};\n    \\draw[thick,->] (0,0) -- (240:1.20)\n      node[left=3pt] {$\\widehat z_3$};\n    \\draw[thin] (-60:.47) arc[start angle=-60,end angle=60,radius=.47];\n    \\node at (.91,-.69) {$2\\pi/3$};\n    \\node at (1.79,.60) {$S_1$};\n    \\node at (-1.50,1.32) {$S_2$};\n    \\node at (-1.50,-1.32) {$S_3$};\n    \\fill (0,0) circle[radius=1.2pt];\n  \\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A planar partition of a rectangular region into three unbounded sectors $S_1$, $S_2$, and $S_3$ meeting at the origin, each with opening angle $2\\pi/3$. The boundary separating $S_2$ and $S_3$ is the negative horizontal half-axis (pointing left). The boundary between $S_1$ and $S_2$ extends upwards and to the right at angle $\\pi/3$. The boundary between $S_1$ and $S_3$ extends downwards and to the right at angle $-\\pi/3$. An arc indicating the angle $2\\pi/3$ is shown between the negative horizontal axis and the boundary between $S_1$ and $S_3$. Three bold arrows originate from the origin pointing along the bisectors of the sectors: $\\hat{z}_1$ points horizontally to the right into $S_1$; $\\hat{z}_2$ points into $S_2$ at angle $2\\pi/3$; and $\\hat{z}_3$ points into $S_3$ at angle $4\\pi/3$. The sectors are labeled $S_1$, $S_2$, and $S_3$ respectively."
    },
    {
      "caption": "$$\\int_{\\Delta_s}h_s(u,v)\\,du\\,dv =\\underbrace{\\frac{(3s)^2}{2}}_{\\text{area}} \\underbrace{\\frac{3s+0+0}{3}}_{\\text{mean affine height}} =\\frac{27s^3}{6}.$$ For fixed $T = s > 0$, the positive integrand is supported, within $u ≤ s$ and $v ≤ s$, on $Δ_{s} = {(u,v) : u ≤ s, v ≤ s, s + u + v ≥ 0}$. Here $h_{s}(u,v) = s + u + v$ is an affine height, with the displayed values at the vertices and barycenter. The shading marks the integration region, not the support or density of the Gaussian pair $(U,V)$. Multiplying the unweighted integral by the density bound $M$ gives the estimate used in the proof. At $s = 0$ the triangle degenerates and the integral is zero.",
      "figure_id": "fig:residual-score-triangle",
      "pdf_page": 12,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:residual-score-triangle",
          "exact_quote": "For fixed $T=s>0$, the positive integrand is supported,\n  within $u\\le s$ and $v\\le s$, on\n  $\\Delta_s=\\{(u,v):u\\le s,\\ v\\le s,\\ s+u+v\\ge0\\}$.\n  Here $h_s(u,v)=s+u+v$ is an affine height, with the displayed values at\n  the vertices and bary",
          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.10cm,y=1.10cm,line cap=round,line join=round,\n                      font=\\small]\n    \\fill[blue!10] (-2,1) -- (1,1) -- (1,-2) -- cycle;\n    \\draw[gray!70,->] (-2.66,0) -- (1.80,0) node[right] {$u$};\n    \\draw[gray!70,->] (0,-2.62) -- (0,1.76) node[above] {$v$};\n    \\draw[thick] (-2,1) -- (1,1) -- (1,-2) -- cycle;\n    \\node[above=4pt,align=center] at (-2,1)\n      {$(-2s,s)$\\\\[-1pt] $h_s=0$};\n    \\node[above right=3pt,align=left] at (1,1)\n      {$(s,s)$\\\\[-1pt] $h_s=3s$};\n    \\node[below right=3pt,align=left] at (1,-2)\n      {$(s,-2s)$\\\\[-1pt] $h_s=0$};\n    \\node[above=3pt] at (-.90,1) {$v=s$};\n    \\node[right=3pt] at (1,-.80) {$u=s$};\n    \\node[rotate=-45,below=4pt] at (-1.02,.02) {$u+v=-s$};\n    \\node at (.52,.50) {$\\Delta_s$};\n    \\fill (0,0) circle[radius=1.5pt];\n    \\node[above left=3pt,align=right] at (0,0)\n      {$(0,0)$\\\\[-1pt] $h_s=s$};\n  \\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A Cartesian coordinate diagram showing the region $\\Delta_s$ in the $(u, v)$ plane. The horizontal axis is labeled $u$ and the vertical axis is labeled $v$. The region $\\Delta_s$ is a right isosceles triangle shaded in purple/blue. Its boundaries are the horizontal line $v = s$, the vertical line $u = s$, and the hypotenuse along the line labeled $u + v = -s$. The vertices are labeled $(-2s, s)$ with $h_s = 0$ (top-left), $(s, s)$ with $h_s = 3s$ (top-right), and $(s, -2s)$ with $h_s = 0$ (bottom-right). The origin is marked with a point $(0, 0)$ inside the triangle with label $h_s = s$. Below the diagram, the integral formula is displayed: $\\int_{\\Delta_s} h_s(u, v) \\, du \\, dv = \\underbrace{\\frac{(3s)^2}{2}}_{\\text{area}} \\underbrace{\\frac{3s + 0 + 0}{3}}_{\\text{mean affine height}} = \\frac{27s^3}{6}$."
    }
